# Did Beijing's chip policy pull against China's own AI companies?

*Yes, on direction, with five limits: four on how, and one on what Beijing meant by it. Every statement below carries a numbered reference to a saved source.*

Written Nov 23, 2026 (UTC) and made public that day. Revised Nov 27, 2026. The figure that goes with it is in the same folder: figure/beijing_vs_labs.html.

Written by Claude, Anthropic's model. Anthropic has argued in public for controls on chip exports to China (that is from my general knowledge; no saved page is cited for it), so judge this note by its sources.

## The claim tested

Palmer Luckey, in a clip posted on Nov 19, 2026: "China doesn't want NVIDIA to sell GPUs to Chinese companies." [1] And next: "there are Chinese companies that want to buy Nvidia GPUs" [2]. The first sentence is from a machine transcript of the clip (0:11 to 0:16). The second is from the text of the post that carries the clip; the post begins it with the word Like, and so does a second machine transcript. A published transcript of the whole session, at the DealBook Summit 2026, has both sentences in the same words, but ends the first with two more words, like this: "Chinese companies like this. There are Chinese companies that want to buy Nvidia GPUs" [3]. That page does not say how its transcript was made. He goes on, by the machine transcript (0:18 to 0:36): "There's 600,000 H200 GPUs sitting in a port right now", because "China will not give them an import permit", "because they want to build an internal domestic processor industry, silicon industry, not build one that is dependent on the United States" [1]. Nobody has checked any of this by ear.

**The answer.** From 2024 to 2026 the Chinese state's declared policy, and what its regulators did, pressed China's AI companies to move away from Nvidia chips. The companies went on using those chips, ordering them and asking for them, while they also bought, used and trained on home-made chips, by their own and their suppliers' accounts (points 9 and 20). Five limits apply. First, Beijing was not one actor: its security and planning side pressed against the chips; its commerce and foreign-affairs side was more open to them, and so, on the record, were some city and county governments, which welcomed Nvidia or approved and offered computing on its chips, and units of the telecom carriers, which used it (points 5 and 17). Second, the pressure came as public warnings, as unpublished guidance and as published texts that push toward home-made chips without barring Nvidia's (provinces' and cities' shares, a region's energy-use quotas, a condition for a national designation as a city's reply describes it, a standards body's guide; points 3, 4 and 18), not as a published ban; the state-media warnings found came mostly in one burst, from Jul 31 to Sept 24, 2025 (23 of 34), with a smaller group from Dec 9, 2025 to Jan 14, 2026, in reports on the American easing for the H200 (6 of 34; the counts are in points 4 and 18). Third, Nvidia's own filings report licensed sales to China, and Reuters reported, citing people it did not name, that some purchases were approved (point 19). Fourth, the companies had reasons of their own, and American curbs and conditions pushed too, and are named as the cause, in the filings and other records the project holds, far more often than a Chinese act is (points 11 and 20): Nvidia's reports since November 2025 say "we are already effectively foreclosed from the China market by U.S. export controls" [4], and of the 3,444,160 chips in the licence applications that Washington's summary of May 11, 2026 records, 1,888,950 (55%; the sum is mine) were in applications it returned without action; its table gives no reason, and the committee that released it says the summary does not show every application in full [5] [6]. Fifth, what Beijing meant by it is not settled: the US-China Economic and Security Review Commission, in its report to Congress, saw in the move against Nvidia of China's market regulator, the State Administration for Market Regulation, which came as the regulator dropped an antitrust probe into Google, "China's willingness to both exert leverage and ease pressure as a negotiating tactic" [7], while the US Trade Representative, Jamieson Greer, asked whether China would let its companies buy the H200, said it was "gonna be a sovereign decision for China" (captions, not checked by ear, at 5:59 in the recording) [8]. The direction of the policy stands under each of the three readings of what Beijing meant by it (point 21).

**How to read the references.** Each number leads to one saved source in the list at the end, where each entry begins with its kind. On the web page a filled number is a document, a filing, a named person on the record, a published commentary, report or statement of state media, an industry body or a research institute, an institution's own notice, or a named outlet's account of a document. An open number is a press report that cites unnamed people, a research firm's, broker's or research group's count, or a caption track or machine transcript; machine captions of a broadcast are marked as such, with the time in the recording, and nobody has checked them by ear. Words between quotation marks were found in the saved copy by a script. English in round brackets after Chinese or Japanese is my rendering, not an official translation.

## 1. The state's declared line: use home-made chips

**1.** **The leader.** Xi Jinping said in 2016 that the internet's core technology is China's greatest weak point and that having core technology controlled by others is its greatest hidden danger: "互联网核心技术是我们最大的“命门”，核心技术受制于人是我们最大的隐患" [9]. In the same speech he said that an internet company, however large, whose core components depend heavily on foreign countries and whose supply chain's weak point is in others' hands is like a house built on someone else's foundation: "那就好比在别人的墙基上砌房子" [10]. In April 2025 he told the Politburo to concentrate forces on high-end chips: "集中力量攻克高端芯片、基础软件等核心技术" [11]. In October 2025 the Central Committee's recommendations for the next five-year plan called for extraordinary measures, "采取超常规措施", in integrated circuits and other fields [12]. The line held at the top in 2026, in the same general words: the government work report of Mar 5, 2026 calls for building integrated circuits into an emerging pillar industry, "打造集成电路、航空航天、生物医药、低空经济等新兴支柱产业" [13], and the five-year plan outline asks for an independently controllable software and hardware ecosystem for computing, "加快培育自主可控、协同运行的软硬件生态" [14]. Neither names a foreign firm or says what companies may buy, and the same work report promises to "积极扩大进口" (expand imports actively) [13]. The speech of 2016 sets the open half of the line beside the other: "最关键最核心的技术要立足自主创新、自立自强" (the most crucial core technologies must rest on independent innovation and self-reliance), but independent innovation "不是关起门来搞研发" (is not research behind closed doors), and the task is to tell which technologies "可以引进但必须安全可控" (may be brought in but must be secure and controllable), which may be absorbed, which developed with others and which China must make by its own strength [10]; the two halves stand together in 2026 too, in Xi's words to the Jiangsu delegation to the National People's Congress on Mar 5, by Xinhua's account of Mar 12, that China's technology must, "在国际上开展合作的同时，坚持独立自主、自立自强" (while cooperating internationally, hold to independence and self-reliance) [15], and in the State Council's regulation on the security of industrial and supply chains, published Mar 31, 2026, which sets "推进高水平对外开放" (advancing high-level opening up) beside "提升产业链供应链安全可控水平" (raising the security and controllability of industrial and supply chains) [16]. A search of People's Daily Online's database of his speeches and activities for Nvidia, its chief executive's name, the H20 and the H200 found nothing, while the same search finds American firms' names: Microsoft and Intel in his own speech of 2016 [9], and Qualcomm once, in the Xinhua report of his meeting of Mar 27, 2024 that People's Daily carried, among the American guests who spoke [17]; the search's totals count more than that (of its 38 items for Microsoft, 21 hold the word only in a font name; of its 8 for Qualcomm, 7 hold the two characters inside other words). The database lacks at least one of his meetings of the period: of 156 of his items of 2023 to November 2026 found in Xinhua's and Qiushi's own lists by words on chips, computing power, AI, American firms or curbs, it holds 150, and the only speech, meeting, letter or article among the six it lacks is his meeting of May 14, 2026 with American executives [18], the other five being joint statements; of 1,512 further Xinhua items read in full, 124 hold no such word but AI; 21 of them are among the 156 (every eighth of the 124, and five joint statements), and Xinhua's own text of the other 103 names no chip, no Nvidia, no H20 or H200 and not Nvidia's chief executive. So this is a strong negative, not a complete one (the searches, with their controls, are in tables/xi_searches.tsv).

**2.** **The rules.** Notices of the finance and industry ministries, published on Dec 26, 2023, require Party and government bodies at township level and above, the public institutions directly under Party committees and governments at those levels, and the departments' public institutions that provide support to those bodies, to write into their purchase requirements for desktop computers and general-purpose servers that the processor and the operating system meet the security-and-reliability evaluation: "应当将CPU、操作系统符合安全可靠测评要求纳入采购需求" [19] [20]. The central state bodies' bulk-purchase standards of March 2024 still kept one desktop and one laptop configuration without it, by Guancha's account (Mar 26, 2024) of the notice that updated their configuration standards for desktop and laptop computers (国机采〔2024〕7号; the notice is held in a university-hosted printout of the central purchasing site's page, without its annexes [21]): the two name Intel or AMD processors and a Windows 10 edition, and "这一配置在“关键部件安全”部分没有要求满足安全可靠测评，但采购单位选择此项配置应符合相关管理要求" (this configuration does not require the evaluation in its key-component security part, but a buyer that chooses it must meet the relevant management requirements) [22]. The desktop standard's 2026 edition, as a university's purchasing site holds the annex, has six configurations and no seventh: Loongson, Phytium, Zhaoxin, Huawei Kunpeng, Hygon and Sunway processors, every one held to the evaluation, "是否为进口产品 否 否 否 否 否 否" (imported product: no, in all six) [23]. The two evaluation centres' results of May 26, 2026, on one centre's own site, list nine AI training and inference chips that passed, every one from a maker registered in China [24]; the evaluation guide says only an entity registered in China may apply, "送测单位应为中国境内注册的实体" [25], and the first matter it evaluates in a product is "设计、开发、生产等关键环节在中国境内完成的情况" (whether the key stages of design, development and production were done in China) [25]; their results of Sept 20, 2026 add two more, HiSilicon's Ascend 950 and Kunlunxin's M100 [26]. Firms apply of their own accord, and firms and users choose whether to use the results: "测评结果由企业和用户自主选择使用" [25]. Some buyers chose to make it a condition for AI chips in 2026, in their own notices: China Post's Shandong branch, in purchase requirements put out for comment on Jun 18, asks for AI servers with "配置不低于8颗国产自研架构AI芯片" (at least eight home-made AI chips of a self-developed architecture), whose accelerator cards, if results for such cards are published before the bids close, "须通过安全可靠测评" (must pass the evaluation) [27]; Shanghai Pudong Development Bank, in a call for suppliers of four pilot servers open from Sept 9, as a payments news site reproduces it, asks for GPU chips that meet the evaluation's "“人工智能训练推理芯片”品类要求" (requirements of the AI training and inference chip category) [28]; and a unit of China Southern Power Grid, in a tender of Nov 16 that accepts imported products through their makers' offices in China, asks the AI card modules of one lot to meet the requirements of the evaluation guide [29]. The record now holds 13 further procurement cases from 2026 in which buyers proposed, set or scored terms tied to the evaluation for AI chips, accelerator cards or GPUs (searches found them: the counts of notices in this note are not a census, and say nothing of how common each kind of notice is), among them China Post Group's January pre-tender consultation, which proposed that accelerator cards or modules in servers offered by bidders "须通过安全可靠测评" (must pass the evaluation) if the China Information Security Evaluation Center officially publishes the relevant results on its website from the day the tender announcement is published through the day before bids are due [30]; a shipbuilding group's institute, for which any GPU "须在中国信息安全测评中心、国家保密科技测评中心这两个网站上公布的型号选取" (must be a model the two centres' sites publish) [31]; the tender on Baosight Software's behalf, with documents on sale from Jun 23 [32]; China Southern Power Grid's digital grid group, with bid submissions opening Jun 29 and a separate clause for imported key products [33]; China Tower's Sichuan branch, Oct 15, whose GPU's processor "必须是中国信息安全测评中心《安全可靠测评结果公告》中所列《人工智能训练推理芯片》中的产品" [34]; and Beijing Anzhen Hospital, with bids due Nov 13, which scores bids on accelerator cards with proof of passing it, as a marked indicator worth points and not as a condition of the bid [35]. Several of these buyers are firms or a bank, not government bodies, among them China Post's group and its Shandong branch, units of China Southern Power Grid, China Tower's Sichuan branch, Baosight Software and Shanghai Pudong Development Bank. The finance ministry, in answers on a notice of its own on medical devices from the European Union (the page's address is dated Jul 10, 2025), put the purchases of firms the state owns outside government purchasing: "国有企业采购不属于政府采购范围，《通知》不适用于国有企业采购。" (state-owned enterprises' purchases are not within the scope of government purchasing, and the notice does not apply to them) [36].  The purchase notices of 2023 ask the evaluation only of the processor and the operating system. Where home-made and other products compete in one government purchase, home-made products get a 20% price preference in the evaluation: "对本国产品的报价给予20%的价格扣除" [37]. The finance and industry ministries' opinions of Dec 15, 2025 put the standard into government purchasing from Jan 1, 2026, "确保于2026年1月1日起在政府采购中落实政策要求" (to make sure the policy's requirements are carried out in government purchasing from Jan 1, 2026) [38]. An imported product meets a gate before any such competition: by the finance ministry's measures of Dec 27, 2007 on government purchases of imported products, purchasing "应当采购本国产品，确需采购进口产品的，实行审核管理" (shall buy home-made products, and buying an imported one, where really needed, is subject to review), an imported product being one "通过中国海关报关验放进入中国境内且产自关境外的产品" (cleared through China's customs into China and produced outside its customs territory) [39]. The test is where a product is made (and, once shares set product by product are in force, whether a set share of its components, by cost, is made in China, and, for particular products, "特定产品的关键组件、关键工序相关要求" (requirements on key components and key processes) [38]), not who owns its maker: state, private and foreign-funded firms "平等享受对本国产品的政府采购支持政策" (enjoy the purchasing support for home-made products equally), and regions and departments may not, in government purchasing, name a brand or restrict where a brand is registered or who owns it [37]. Xi himself told representatives of international business on Mar 28, 2025 that China would see that products foreign-funded firms make in China take part equally, by law, in government purchasing, "保障外资企业在华生产的产品依法平等参与政府采购" [40]; the words cover products made in China, not imported chips. A tender that allows no imported products can still be awarded to servers that carry Nvidia chips: a tender document of March 2025 for the University of the Chinese Academy of Sciences, issued by the purchasing centre for central state bodies, asks "是否允许进口产品" [41] (are imported products allowed), answers 否 (no) and gives its four GPU servers' origin as home-made; the award of Apr 24, 2025 includes four Dell servers, each with "1*NVIDIA HGX H20 8-GPU SXM 96GB 500W GPUs模组" [42] (one NVIDIA HGX H20 eight-GPU module). An award is a contract decision, not a delivery. Three further notices found that take no imports name Nvidia's models themselves: a Shanghai district's government cloud, June 2025, lists "T4GPU8 块" (eight T4 GPUs) among its resources beside "本项目不采购进口设备" [43]; a county police bureau in Henan asks, Dec 4, 2025, "至少8 块NVIDIA T4-16G 高性能运算卡" [44]; and a Henan medical academy, in March 2026, seeks bids to rent server clusters, requires domestically developed brands for the servers, accelerators and supporting software, and lists this minimum GPU specification "NVIDIA HGX H100 8-GPU(H100 80GB SXM5)" [45]; all three are tenders, not awards. Some central buyers' notices of November 2026 ask for home-made computing outright: the drug regulator's medical-device evaluation centre rented an "8卡国产人工智能算力环境" (an eight-card home-made AI computing environment) and asked for its existing applications to be moved "从原人工智能计算架构向国产人工智能计算架构" (from the original AI computing architecture to a home-made one), the original not named [46]; Peking University sought eight inference cards, imports not accepted, for work "基于国产AI算力" (on home-made AI computing) [47]. The record now holds 48 further buyers' notices found, from August 2023 to November 2026, of government bodies, universities, hospitals, state groups, banks and insurers, that ask for home-made computing, chips or processors, exclude imported products, or buy by a home-made maker's name, among them Zhoukou's health commission, for the city's hospitals of grade two and above, "高性能国产化算力集群" (a high-performance home-made computing cluster), imports not accepted [48]; East China Normal University, whose training server must take "配置8块国产GPU" (eight home-made GPUs) as a starred term [49]; the China Foreign Exchange Trade System, "配置至少 8 颗国产人工智能处理器" (at least eight home-made AI processors a server) [50]; China Cinda, to replace all its AI computing, "实现公司现有AI算力的国产化全量替代" [51]; Nanjing's police, "采购四台国产化智能算力服务器" [52]; and China News Service, "国产高性能GPU算力卡" [53].

**3.** **The targets, and the money.** Nine of China's 31 provincial-level governments were found to set a share for home-made computing, each by its plan's own measure: chips, equipment, hardware and software, or computing capacity; one of the nine, Zhejiang's, is for the computing of one sector, its information and communications industry, by 2030, as the target of a project in that sector's five-year plan [54]. Two are worded as aims, with the word 力争 (strive to): Sichuan's and Guangdong's. Jiangsu's own table marks its figures as indicative, not binding [55]. The sentences that set the shares of Shanghai, Tianjin, Hebei and Inner Mongolia carry no such word or label, and Shandong's plan is known only from press accounts. Shanghai's two plans of July 2026 for the next five years set no home-made share [56]; the digital one sets a share of green power, "新建智算中心绿电占比达80%以上" (80% or more in new intelligent-computing centres) [57], while a deputy director of the city's economy commission spoke in January 2026 of building a "国芯国模国云" system (home-made chips, models and clouds), with no figure [58]. Shanghai's texts of 2025 aim and advise rather than require: the economy commission's opinions on the intelligent-computing cloud industry of Mar 24, 2025 (沪经信软〔2025〕160号) aim, for 2027, at "智算规模力争达到200EFLOPS，其中自主可控算力占比超70%" (striving for 200 EFLOPS of intelligent computing, over 70% of it independently controllable) [59], and its guide for building intelligent-computing centres, of January 2025, makes a baseline figure for energy efficiency the entry requirement for a new centre and only advises on chips, "宜采用自主可控的 AI 训练及推理加速卡" (independently controllable AI training and inference accelerator cards should be used): the words 国产 and 自主可控 stand in it twice each, each time in such advice [60]. Of the other 22, 20 have a plan that was read and sets no share (Chongqing's plan of 2026 sets shares only for green power and for the uptake of new smart terminals and agents [61]). Beijing city's plan aims, by 2027, at an independently controllable full stack of hardware and software for intelligent computing and "具备100%自主可控智算中心建设能力" (the ability to build intelligent-computing centres that are 100% independently controllable) [62]; Guizhou's aims at a national rank, "国产智算能力位居全国前列" [63]; Shaanxi's computing item sets no share; on building, it says it will "按需建设算力基础设施" (build computing infrastructure as needed) [64]. Tibet's plan is known only from accounts of it, its issuer's own among them, whose targets name no home-made hardware [65], and for Xinjiang no computing plan's text was found, though the region's development and reform commission, in a reply posted Jul 24, 2026, says two were issued, "印发《绿色低碳的算力网建设实施方案（2025—2027年）》《自治区绿色算力中心布局方案》" (issued the plan for building a green and low-carbon computing network, 2025 to 2027, and the region's layout plan for green computing centres) [66]; and Hainan's AI plan, one of the 20, was published abridged, "（此件公开发布，此文有删减）" (this document is published with cuts) [67], so a clause left out of it cannot be ruled out. Texts that push home-made chips in computing and set no share are more common: the record now holds eight more of local governments, among them Beijing city's AI plan for 2023 to 2025, which tells the city to "积极引导大模型研发企业应用国产人工智能芯片" (actively guide large-model developers to use home-made AI chips) [68], and Shanghai's report on its plan for 2024, made to the city's congress on the city government's behalf, which names a project to change the chips of intelligent-computing centres, "实施智算中心“换芯”工程", with no number or date [69]. One of the eight is the first published text found that ties energy-use quotas to home-made computing: measures of six bodies of Inner Mongolia's regional government to promote the token economy, published on Sept 1, 2026, addressed to the region's leagues, cities, departments, firms and institutions and carried on a banner government's site, promise "支持企业采用国产化算力建设超大规模智算中心，在项目用地、绿电、能耗指标等方面给予保障" (to support firms that build very large intelligent-computing centres on home-made computing, with land, green power and energy-use quotas assured) [70]; they are a region's measures of support, which offer that support to firms using home-made computing to build very large intelligent-computing centres and set no required share. The kind of plan that holds Zhejiang's share, each provincial communications administration's five-year plan for its sector, was then searched as a class for the other 30 units: the plan for 2026 to 2030 was read in full for ten (Shanghai's and Shandong's in trade sites' copies) and as a consultation draft for two, only the plan for 2021 to 2025 for seven more, and for eleven no plan text was found; none of the texts read sets a home-made share (Beijing's, for one, asks to "加大力度适配国产算力芯片" (step up adaptation to home-made computing chips) [71], with no figure), so the nine remain a count of what was found, not a census. The table below gives each of the nine shares in its document's own words; the 31 rows are in tables/provinces_31.tsv, beside this note.

| Government | Share set | In the document's words | Who issued it, and when |
|---|---|---|---|
| Jiangsu | 70% or more of chips in newly built computing centres by 2030 (50% by 2025); marked indicative | "新建算力中心国产算力芯片使用占比达 70%以上" | four provincial bodies; signed Apr 22, 2024 (苏通联〔2024〕7号), by the issuing notice, which the PDF does not carry [55] [72] |
| Shanghai | over 50% of chips in newly built intelligent-computing centres by 2025 | "本市新建智算中心国产算力芯片使用占比超过50%" | the city's plan for 2024 and 2025, as carried by Yicai [73] and, with its issuing notice, by the Shandong Big Data Bureau's site on a page dated Apr 1, 2024; by that page's title the plan was issued by the Shanghai Communications Administration with other bodies in Shanghai, eleven in all [74] |
| Tianjin | over 60% of chips in the city's computing centres by 2026 | "全市算力中心国产算力芯片使用占比超过 60％" | the city government's general office, signed Jul 1, 2024 and issued Jul 18, 2024 [75] |
| Guangdong | 70% of newly added computing by the end of 2025, an aim | "力争到2025年底，新增国产化算力占比达到70%" | the Guangdong Communications Administration and eight other bodies in the province, Mar 7, 2024 [76] |
| Hebei | 60% or more of newly added computing hardware and software independently controllable, by 2025 | "新增算力基础软硬件设施自主可控比例60%以上" | the provincial government's general office, May 8, 2024 [77] |
| Sichuan | over 50% of computing equipment, an aim | "力争国产化算力设备占比超过50%" | the provincial development and reform commission and three other bodies, Nov 15, 2024 [78] |
| Inner Mongolia | 50% or more of computing by 2025, among the goals for the region's national computing hub | "国产化算力占比达50%以上" | the regional government's general office, Oct 10, 2023 [79] |
| Shandong | 90% of computing by 2025 | "国产化算力将达90%" | a plan of 2022, by the provincial daily's account on the provincial government's site [80] |
| Zhejiang | over 60% of the computing of one sector, the province's information and communications industry, by 2030; the target of a project in that sector's five-year plan | "到2030年，浙江信息通信行业算力规模达150EFLOPS，国产化算力占比超60%" | the Zhejiang Communications Administration and the provincial development and reform commission, posted Jul 29, 2026 [54] |

Cities, districts and provinces also pay part of the rent for computing through vouchers. Counting one rule in force for each local government whose text is saved in a form that could be checked by script and which states a share of the bill, 8 of the 93 found by searches that went province by province and city by city, saved and read (not a census of every local government), pay more for home-made computing and still pay for other computing, 4, those of Sichuan province, Ningbo city, Huangyan district and the Changsha Economic and Technological Development Zone, pay for home-made computing only, and 81 state no higher rate for home-made computing: 80 of them pay one rate whatever the chip, and one, Hangzhou's, pays 30% or 20% by a type of computing facility that it does not name; the rate counted is the one for an ordinary applicant, and some rules pay a named kind of firm more, whatever the chip. The class looks at that one payment only: 9 of the 81 also give home-made computing something by another provision of the same rule, 6 money to build, buy or run home-made computing facilities or equipment (Shanghai's building support and Beijing E-Town's support for servers with home-made chips, both given below; the Lingang Special Area's subsidy of up to 10% of the purchase of computing equipment whose chips' key core technology is independently controllable [81]; Songjiang district's matching of up to 30% for projects deploying independent intelligent-computing facilities [82]; Fuzhou's operating subsidy of 50,000 yuan a year for each petaflop sold or used, for centres at least 80% of whose rated computing runs on independent chips [83]; and Hebei district of Tianjin's up to 50% of the research equipment for independently controllable research platforms built with the Tianjin AI Computing Center [84]), 3 an award for adapting to, or building on, home-made chips or computing (Shenzhen's up to 50% of a firm's cost of moving its models to home-made AI technology [85], Guizhou's 30% of the contract for adapting on home-made computing, with its own ceiling of 1.5 million yuan a year [86], and Hebei district's up to 20% of research costs for work built on Ascend and other independently controllable routes [84]), and 1, Beijing city's, the condition on its suppliers given below; 8 if that condition and Hebei district's research award are left out; none of the 8 that pay more or of the 4 that pay for home-made computing only gives home-made computing anything by another provision. That is a premium for home-made computing beside the payment; it does not change the payment's own terms, which name no chip, though Hebei district's pays only for computing bought from one named centre, which Tianjin Daily says runs home-made computing on Huawei's Ascend architecture [84] [87]. What is counted is a rule that pays a share of a firm's bill for rented or bought computing: most call the payment a computing voucher (算力券) or a like name, and 38 of the 93 give it another name, a subsidy, a reward or support. These are the rules found by searches that went province by province and city by city, saved and read, not a census of every local government, and the figure lists the 93: the searches covered the 31 province-level governments, 24 named cities and new areas, and, in four searches of Nov 25, 2026 that went city by city, each with a second pass, the cities, districts and zones on their lists in the east, south, north, and centre and west; other districts and counties only as they turned up. Of the 93, 52 are read on an official page or file of the issuing government or body and 41 in another copy (another body's site, a news or commercial site, or an Internet Archive capture). Some favour home-made computing by their rates (of the eight that pay more, seven word the favoured kind 自主 or 自主可控, independent or independently controllable, in the rate sentence, and Binhai New Area's rule specifies independent Xinchuang computing (自主信创); of the four that pay for one kind only, Sichuan's, Ningbo's and the Changsha zone's say 国产, home-made, and Huangyan's 自主; two counted at one rate name home-made computing, or adapting to home-made systems and chips, in the rate sentence only at that rate: Binhaiwan New Area's pays up to the same 50% for adapting to home-made systems and chips as for computing, "国产操作系统适配、国产芯片适配" [88], and Haidian district's encourages large-model firms to use home-made computing and pays up to 50% of the settled amount, "鼓励大模型企业使用国产算力，按照实际结算额给予最高50%的补贴" [89], a rate its application guide applies to contracts for GPU computing [90]; and Hebei district of Tianjin pays one rate, by its words, for one named centre, which Tianjin Daily calls wholly home-made [84] [87]; and Shenyang's rule, counted at one rate, names no origin, while its province's data bureau describes the city's voucher in three replies, in four sentences that do not agree, one of them as for users of local home-made computing, "对使用本地国产算力资源的用户" [91]); every such rule found that is in force, four apart, still pays for other computing too, at a lower rate, though Binjiang district, by its bureau's account, added a subsidy for other computing only with its policy issued in May 2025 [92]. Of the 8, Chengdu's revised voucher rules, in force from May 13, 2026, set ceilings of 50% for independently controllable and 30% for other intelligent computing, "自主可控智能算力不超过服务费用的50%、非自主可控智能算力不超过服务费用的30%" [93], and Longwan district and Wenzhou Bay New Area carry out Wenzhou's 30% and 10% in rules in force from Jun 21, 2026 [94]; two date from the autumn of 2026: Hubei province's scheme, signed Sept 30, 2026, pays 20% of the actual cost and 30% for independently controllable computing, "企事业单位按实际发生费用的20％予以补贴，其中购买自主可控算力服务的，按实际发生费用的30％予以补贴" [95] (its voucher of 2025, under the province's measures , offered a subsidy of 10% of eligible actual spending on intelligent computing and supercomputing, capped at RMB 1 million per applicant per year, with no chip condition stated in the notice posted on the Qianjiang city government's site, "算力券按照 用算主体智算、超算 算力资源 实际 使用费用的10％予以补贴" [96]), and the Wuhan Data Bureau's voucher measures, in force from Oct 29, 2026 for one year, pay the same two rates [97]; Wuhan's city measures of February 2025, in force until Mar 18, 2027, pay 50% of the bill, up to 200,000 yuan, whatever the chip (not counted: the Data Bureau's measures are the rule counted for Wuhan) [98]. Hangzhou, whose measures of July 2024 paid 30% for home-made and 20% for other facilities [99], no longer states such a tier: the city government's plan of 2025, in force from Jul 15, 2025, repealed those measures, "杭政办函〔2024〕40号）自本方案施行之日起同时废止", and pays 30% or 20% "按照算力设施的不同类型" (by the type of computing facility), without saying which type gets which [100]. Of the four rules that pay for home-made computing only, one is Ningbo's: the city data bureau's rules for 2026, signed Mar 16, 2026, issue vouchers of up to 50% of each period's bill for contracts that name home-made computing, "合同服务内容需明确为国产算力服务" [101], as its rules for 2025 did, at 50% of each period's bill, for buyers with a need for home-made computing, "具有国产算力使用需求" [102]; the rule is yearly, the fourth of its voucher periods for 2026 ending on Dec 31, 2026, and it is read on a commercial policy platform's page that carries the notice whole. Another is Sichuan's: its measures for the computing network, reported on Aug 13, 2026 [103] and issued by the provincial development and reform commission as 川发改数据〔2026〕338号, written Aug 11 and published Aug 13, 2026 [104], set up a voucher for what firms spend buying home-made computing, "对企业购买国产算力费用给予支持", up to 20% (30% for start-ups founded within three years, 40% for one-person AI firms), and name no voucher for other computing; their separate token voucher, for model calls and AI services, names no chip [105]. Securities Daily's report of the same day, as Eastmoney carries it, prints the same sentence on the voucher, "对企业购买国产算力费用给予支持", without the general rate [106]. The other two are a district's and a zone's: Huangyan district's policy of Jul 16, 2025 (黄政发〔2025〕18号) pays 50% of what firms spend buying 自主 (independent) intelligent computing for their own research, "企业采购自主智能算力服务的，其研发投入（委外除外）中的算力成本部分，按照智算服务实际采购金额的50%给予算力补贴" [107], and names no rate for other computing; the Changsha Economic and Technological Development Zone's measures of Mar 23, 2026 pay 10% of what firms actually spend renting home-made intelligent computing for agent applications, up to 1 million yuan, "对租用国产智能算力" [108], "按年度实际发生金额的10%，给予最高100万元补助" [108]. Others pay one rate whatever the chip: Shenzhen's training voucher up to 50% of the contract, 60% for start-ups [85], Qingdao's up to 20% [109], Guizhou's 30% of the contract's valid amount (合同有效金额) for intelligent computing, under measures in force from Apr 1, 2026 [86], Anhui's up to 20% of total computing spend, under its policy of August 2025 (皖政秘〔2025〕108号) [110], and, among the rules read on Nov 25, 2026, Liaoning's up to 30% [111], Heilongjiang's 30%, or 50% for research projects [112], Jiangsu's 50%, in principle a ceiling for province and city together, inside which each city divided into districts (设区市) may set its own rate [113], Fujian's up to 50% [114], Jiangxi's up to 20% [115], Henan's 20% [116], Hunan's up to 30%, under rules whose stated aim includes steering buyers to home-made computing first, "引导算力需求方优先使用国产算力资源" [117], Shandong's up to 10% for each side of a contract [118], Xiamen's up to 50% [119], and Beijing city's innovation voucher of up to 25% for small technology firms, whose article 17 requires a computing supplier to be able to serve on home-made chips with a home-made software stack, "可基于国产芯片并使用国产软件栈提供算力服务", a condition on the supplier, not a higher rate and not a rule that only such service is paid [120]; Shanghai's pays the same rent share for any chip, up to 30% from the city and, with its districts, up to 100% for a year at most for early-stage and small firms, and gives building support of up to 10% to projects deploying 自主 (independent) intelligent-computing facilities [121]. One zone dropped its tier: Beijing's E-Town paid up to 40% and up to 30% under its rule of March 2024, and a later rule repeals that one and pays 30% whatever the chip, keeping up to 10% of the cost of home-made AI chip servers that a cloud firm buys and offers as a service on the zone's computing platform [122]. Beijing city's application guides, now held as its bureau's own pages, pay 20% whatever the chip in 2025 and, in 2026, rates set by kind of applicant, not by chip [123] [124]; the 2026 guide's attached note also refuses support for computing equipment that national departments have declared a security risk or not recommended, and names none [125]. Guides, like Nanjing's at up to 30% [126], are not among the 93. Longgang district of Shenzhen dropped a stated preference: the draft it put out for comment in June 2024 encouraged home-made computing with graded voucher rates [127] [128]; its rules in force from Jun 13, 2025 paid 30% of the computing bill with no condition on the chip [129], and the rules that replaced them on Jul 6, 2026 pay yearly sums in five tiers, again with none [130]. The spokesman of the state planner, the National Development and Reform Commission (国家发展改革委), said at the commission's press conference of Sept 29, 2025 (its own transcript [131]; Xinhua reported the words on Oct 5) that it would "将在“两重”建设中支持国产算力、模型、语料库发展" (support the development of home-made computing, models and corpora in the “two majors” programme) and, in the same paragraph, "持续在算力等领域发放“人工智能券”，为企业使用算力服务提供补贴" (keep issuing AI vouchers in computing and other fields, to subsidise firms' use of computing services) [132]. The local rules found are in tables/local_vouchers.tsv and tables/local_vouchers_more.tsv. Two provinces reward data centres whose basic hardware and software are 90% or more home-made: a one-off reward of up to 5 million yuan for new or rebuilt centres in Shanxi [133], and a reward of up to 10 million yuan in Ningxia, by its industry department's account of a regional policy [134], which the region repealed by its decision 宁政规发〔2025〕5号, signed Nov 27, 2025 [135].

**4.** **The agencies and the state's media, in public.** The Ministry of State Security warned in July 2025 that some chips, smart devices and software made abroad may have had backdoors planted on purpose when they were designed and made: "一些境外生产的芯片、智能设备或者软件可能在设计制造阶段就被故意预埋了“后门”" [144]. Its advice on chips is for posts that handle secrets and is worded as a possibility, "重点涉密岗位可通过采用自主可控芯片和国产操作系统" (key posts handling secrets can use independently controllable chips and home-made operating systems) [144]; it names no company and says nothing about what firms should buy. In April 2026 the ministry asked industry to "严控芯片、服务器、工控设备等关键硬件准入" (strictly control the admission of key hardware such as chips, servers and industrial control equipment) against planted spy devices, again naming no maker or country [145]. In January 2026 the secretariat of the national cybersecurity standards committee published a practice guide for AI accelerator chips that says they should not offer remote shutdown or location functions, "不应提供远程关闭、定位和与未声明的通信实体交互数据等功能" [146]; it is guidance, not a binding standard, and the provision names no maker. In May 2026 Li Chao, of the state planner's policy research office, said at its press conference that it was guiding home-made large models to do more to adapt to home-made computing chips: "指导国产大模型加大力度适配国产算力芯片" [147]. State media went further and named the chip. After the cyberspace regulator, the Cyberspace Administration of China, summoned Nvidia, a People's Daily commentary of Aug 1, 2025 said that 'sick' chips must never be allowed on duty: "绝不能让“带病”的芯片上岗" [148]. Yuyuan Tantian, an account of the state broadcaster [149], wrote on Aug 10, 2025 that the H20 was neither green, nor advanced, nor safe, and: "作为消费者，我们当然可以选择，不买" (as consumers we can of course choose not to buy) [150]. Of 297 state and Party media items on Nvidia and its chips found from July 2025 to November 2026, 34 only warn (33 of them about the chips for AI computing; one, a report of a forum, about home-made software and hardware at large), and 23 of the 34 date from Jul 31 to Sept 24, 2025 [151] [148] [152]. One is a week older: China Daily's excerpt, on Jul 24, 2025, of an outside writer's post that calls relying on such chips "a dangerous trap" [153], printed with the line "The views don't necessarily reflect those of China Daily." [153] Ten are later. A China Daily report of Nov 1, 2025 gives a scholar's view, Tu Xinquan's of the University of International Business and Economics, that "such security concerns about Nvidia chips were not unfounded" [154]. Six date from Dec 9, 2025 to Jan 14, 2026 and are reports on the American easing for the H200: China National Radio's "美国允许对华出口英伟达H200高端算力芯片 葫芦里卖的什么药？" (America allows the export of Nvidia's H200 to China: what is it up to?) [155] and Beijing Daily's "美国放宽H200芯片出口中国管制" (the United States eases controls on exporting H200 chips to China) [156], which recall the regulator's summons of July 2025, and four Global Times reports in which analysts speak of security concerns over the chips and of buyers' caution [157] [158] [159] [160]. The last three are Global Times items of 2026: news reports of Mar 3 and Mar 18 in which an industry analyst, Ma Jihua, is reported as adding "that Nvidia still faces unresolved security issues" and as saying that it "must fully demonstrate that its products are safe and have no backdoors" [161] [162], and the paper's own unsigned commentary (Viewpoint) of Nov 6, which calls a return to large-scale purchases "contrary to the most basic principles of commercial logic", for reasons of supply and of US policy; it does not speak of backdoors [163]. Fourteen items mix a warning with a welcome, ten of them after September 2025, the Economic Daily piece quoted below among them. The items after Sept 24, 2025 are what the searches and the scan of the saved pages found, a sample of what these outlets printed and not a full count (the count, how each item was found and the reading of each are in tables/state_media_items.tsv). For January 2024 to June 2025 a baseline gathered by searches and scans of saved pages and outlet lists, not a census, holds 174 items on Nvidia and its chips (tables/state_media_baseline_2024_2025h1.tsv): 160 in outlets the later list covers, and 14 in three People's Daily group papers it does not cover. One only warns: a column credited to People's Posts and Telecommunications News, carried on the site of Outlook Weekly on Mar 13, 2025, which argues against relying on the H20, "对H20芯片的过度依赖，会使中国人工智能产业在全球竞争中陷入被动局面" (over-reliance on the H20 chip will leave China's AI industry in a passive position in global competition) [164]; it speaks of dependence and cost, not of backdoors. None of the 160 comparable items was found to tell buyers not to buy Nvidia’s chips. An industry body had urged caution about American chips in general on Dec 3, 2024: the Internet Society of China called on Chinese companies to be prudent in choosing to buy them, "审慎选择采购美国芯片", giving Washington's export controls as its ground, and in the same sentence to use chips made in China by domestic and foreign-funded firms alike, "积极使用内外资企业在华生产制造的芯片" [165]. Four associations urged caution that day in buying American chips, as one of the four, the China Association of Communications Enterprises, says, "中国通信企业协会、中国互联网协会、中国半导体行业协会、中国汽车工业协会发表声明，呼吁国内企业审慎选择采购美国芯片" [166]: Yicai reported that the automobile manufacturers' association advised Chinese car makers "谨慎采购美国芯片" (to be cautious in buying American chips), "协会建议中国汽车企业谨慎采购美国芯片" [167], and Safety Insider, republishing Outlook's roundup, gives the semiconductor association's "中国相关行业将不得不谨慎采购美国芯片" and the communications enterprises association's "为保障信息通信行业的产业链、供应链安全稳定，应谨慎采购美国芯片" [168]. When Washington cleared the H200, a signed commentary in the Economic Daily said that Chinese users might buy it selectively but would not again pay for the whole lot, "中国用户可能会选择性购买H200芯片，但不会再全盘埋单", and foresaw a market on two tracks, "“多元采购+自主为主”的双轨模式" (buying from many sources, with home-made first) [169].

## 2. What the companies used and wanted: Nvidia

**5.** **A Shanghai government body says so.** Shanghai's Commission of Economy and Informatization wrote, in a reply dated Apr 30, 2025 to two members of the city's political consultative conference, Fang Qizhong and Zhu Minghua (proposal No. 0093), and published on its site on Nov 10, 2025, that in the city the computing clusters that internet companies build themselves, to meet market-oriented firms' demand, run mainly on Nvidia high-performance cards, "互联网企业自建算力集群，满足市场化企业需求，主要以英伟达高性能卡为主", while the three big telecom operators mainly buy Huawei's and other home-made intelligent-computing clusters, "三大运营商主要购置华为等国产化智算集群", to diversify the city's computing [170]; one of the three, China Unicom's Shanghai branch, had from Aug 27, 2024 used a cluster of H800 servers as its only user, by the reply that a supplier's auditor filed in June 2026 (below). The commission repeats the sentence word for word in two more replies published in 2025, and the city's human-resources bureau in a third [171] [172] [173]: one assessment, not four. No other government's reply found says what the internet companies' own clusters run on; Xi'an's science bureau, in a reply of Jul 13, 2026, says the types of computing in that city cover home-made computing and "主流算力（NVIDIA）" (mainstream computing: Nvidia) [174]. Liaoning's data bureau, in a reply of Jun 24, 2026, says that some small and medium firms in the province, driven by profit, "偏好高性能、高性价比的国外先进算力，对国产算力接受度偏低" (prefer high-performance, good-value foreign advanced computing, and accept home-made computing little) [175]. Three more replies of local governments describe local H800 installations or a plan to buy Nvidia servers: Zhongwei's data bureau (October 2023 in the page address), "中国移动数据中心部署算力服务平台，上架100台H800服务器" (China Mobile's data centre has racked 100 H800 servers) [176]; Xuchang's science bureau (Jul 15, 2025), "昇腾算力 150P，英伟达 H800 高性能算力 320P" (150P of Ascend computing, 320P of Nvidia H800) [177]; and Bayannur's development and reform commission (August 2025 in the page address), of a planned centre, "采购英伟达和国产高性能服务器" (to buy Nvidia and home-made high-performance servers) [178]. The chip designer Kunlunxin, which calls itself an AI chip company that China Mobile has invested in, says on its own site that in the "类CUDA生态" (CUDA-like ecosystem) section of China Mobile's central purchase of inference equipment for 2025 and 2026 it "在三个标包中均取得份额第一" (took the largest share in three lots) [179]. Some of the carriers' own notices, read as tender sites and a tender agent's site carry them, ask for home-made chips by maker or by origin, or label their lots by software ecosystem, and none of the 37 such pages read (notices, one filing and one news page) names Nvidia or an Nvidia model. Two other notices held do. One seeks bids for software services: China Unicom's Shenzhen company, in its second call for software services for its computing in Futian district (with tender documents available from Aug 26, 2026), as a tender site carries it, asks that the software be compatible with "NVIDIA GPU / 昇腾 NPU 异构算力" (Nvidia GPU and Ascend NPU heterogeneous computing) [180]. The other is small: a Jiangsu company that bears China Unicom's name and publishes its tender on China Unicom's procurement site (the notice names no owner), in a tender of Sept 23, 2024 with a budget of 800,000 yuan, asked to rent computing on cards it writes as "NV RTX 3090", "NV RTX 4090" and "NV Tesla A100" [181]. The 37 date from January 2024 to November 2026; four purchases of carriers in 2023 did name Nvidia's chips, by a supplier's filing, a trade site's account of two notices, a broker's account of a notice and a financial adviser's filed account: China Unicom's Qinghai branch contracted 中贝通信 (Zhongbei Communication) to supply "H800算力服务器" (H800 computing servers) for a three-year computing service, by the supplier's notice of Sept 7, 2023 on the disclosure site cninfo.com.cn [182]; China Mobile's Shanghai company, in its tender of Nov 28, 2023, "中国移动上海公司某重要客户算力建设工程服务器设备采购" (the purchase of servers for a computing build for an important customer of China Mobile's Shanghai company), set out to build for that customer a "千卡级H800智算中心" (an H800 intelligent-computing centre of a thousand cards), by the cross-border trade site 大数跨境's account of the notice and of its candidate notice [183]; the candidate notice for the 2023 central purchase of computing services by China Telecom's Ningxia company showed a bid from Youfang Technology in lot 2 for "A800 智算算力服务" (A800 intelligent-computing service), by Huaan Securities' report of Jan 29, 2024 [184]; and China Telecom's Guangdong branch bought from a single source in December 2023, for a customer's private computing pool, 500 servers and "英伟达显卡 500 片" (500 Nvidia cards), GeForce RTX 4070 among them, by the account the financial adviser of 湖南华升 filed in March 2026 [185]. In 2024, too, by Leadtek's account as a Taiwanese paper reports it, China Mobile contracted Leadtek's Shanghai subsidiary for a data centre with "200台輝達（NVIDIA）HGX A800 AI伺服器" (200 Nvidia HGX A800 AI servers) [186]; China Unicom's Shanghai branch was, during the reporting period, the only customer using a cluster built by a company controlled by Fujian state capital, with H800 servers as its core nodes and peak computing power of 2,000P under sparse FP16 computation, under a 36-month cloud contract of 362.568 million yuan starting Aug 27, 2024, by Shanghai Damingcheng's reply reproduced in its auditor's June 2026 filing, "名城数字向智算公司采购的相关算力集群的唯一使用客户为中国联合网络通信有限公司上海市分公司" (the only customer using the cluster is China Unicom's Shanghai branch) [187]; and Zhongwei's data bureau's reply above says that China Telecom's and China Mobile's data centres there had racked 20 and 100 H800 servers [176]. Of the 37: China Mobile's Yunnan company, buying for a customer's computing project, required on Feb 26, 2026 the authorization of a chip maker that "应为国内主流GPU芯片制造商" (must be a mainstream domestic GPU maker), and named twelve [188]; China Mobile labels the lots of its central purchases "CANN生态" (the CANN ecosystem; a table in the US-China commission's report of 2026 sets "Huawei’s CANN" beside "Nvidia’s CUDA" [189]) [190] or "类CUDA生态" (CUDA-like, which names no maker) [191]; a broker, relaying a WeChat account, gives reported award amounts for China Mobile's 2025–26 inference-equipment tender by accelerator card, starting with "昇腾 30 亿、昆仑芯 12 亿" (Ascend 3 billion yuan, Kunlunxin 1.2 billion) and T-Head, Iluvatar CoreX and Biren after them, no Nvidia among them [192]; and China Telecom's tender for its N-series supernode servers, in a copy a tender site listed on Nov 17, 2026 (an estimated 5,248 servers; the copy masks the tender's number and leaves its dates and price ceiling blank), accepts only "来自中华人民共和国的产品" (products from the PRC) and asks for chips of "模组形态、NPU架构" (in module form, of an NPU architecture), naming no maker [193]; a second copy, which another tender site listed on Nov 16, prints the wider clause instead, "投标产品应是来自中华人民共和国或是与中华人民共和国有正常贸易与往来的国家或地区的产品" (products from the PRC or from countries or regions trading normally with it) [194], and which copy gives the notice's own words cannot be told from them; its tenders of July 2024 and, in Jiangsu, of September 2026 also accepted products of countries or regions trading normally with China [195] [196]. Three more tenders of China Telecom companies, of October 2025 to March 2026 (Tianyi Cloud's Guangdong branch, the Changji branch, and 中电信翼智), as the national public-resources platform carries them, give the PRC and countries or regions with which it has normal trade and dealings as eligible origins for mature, stable products in the saved text [197] [198] [199]. At least 10 of the 37 ask for nothing of the kind: they name no chip maker or chip model, no home-made chip and no software ecosystem, and set no rule on where products come from (counted by script over the saved pages), China Mobile's central tenders for "人工智能服务器7994台" (7,994 AI servers) for 2024 and 2025 [200] and for "人工智能超节点合计20480卡" (AI supernodes of 20,480 cards in all) for 2026 and 2027 [201] among them, which leave the specification to their tender documents; the AI-server tender of Oct 31, 2025 of a digital-technology company that bears China Unicom's name (联通数字科技有限公司) and three affiliates let a maker "注册地在中华人民共和国境外" (registered outside the PRC) bid through an agent [202], which China Unicom's tender of Jul 24, 2026 for 123 AI servers does not, taking no bids from agents for them, "本项目人工智能服务器设备、集成安装服务不接受代理商投标" (this project takes no bids from agents for the AI servers or the integration and installation services) [203]; and China Mobile's Hebei company, in its notice of Jul 16, 2025 for 30 servers, marks 25 of them "国产芯片（非标产品）" (home-made chips, non-standard products) only in a table that the notice sets aside, "以下表格系统生成，不作为参考" (the table below is generated by the system and is not for reference) [204]. Beyond the notices, the record now holds 16 cases of carriers' computing on home-made chips (centres, clusters, plans and purchases) and 13 of carriers' computing that holds, offers or buys Nvidia hardware (counted by script): a deputy general manager of China Unicom's Qinghai green-computing company, of its cluster with Alibaba Cloud, "核心GPU环节国产化率、集群云化率均达100%" (the core GPUs 100% home-made) [205], and Xinhua, Apr 10, 2025, "据了解，中国移动自有智算中心芯片国产化率已超90%" (it is understood that China Mobile's own centres are over 90% home-made in their chips) [206]; but China Telecom's cloud sells hosts on which "PN8I型云主机采用NVIDIA L20 GPU" (the PN8I cloud host uses the NVIDIA L20 GPU) [207], China Mobile's Hohhot centre has inference cards "NVIDIA显卡30、40系列等" (Nvidia's 30 and 40 series among them), by a named officer of the carrier [208], and its Beijing node in June 2024 was "AI芯片国产化率33%" (33% home-made in its AI chips) [209].

**6.** **The Party's journal, a chip maker and a model maker say why.** An investigation by a joint study group of the Party journal Qiushi's economics desk and the research institute CCID, carried by the National Data Administration in May 2026, found that moving off Nvidia's software is costly and slow: "迁移成本高昂、适配周期漫长" [210]. The record now holds eleven more reports and commentaries on the same question, among them: a researcher of CCID, as China Newsweek reports him (Mar 17, 2025), that some computing that government bought to back home-made substitution sits idle because getting particular models to run on domestic cards takes extensive software adaptation, "为什么很多智算中心的算力被闲置，关键就在这里" (that is the key to why computing capacity at so many intelligent-computing centres sits idle) [211]; Economic Information Daily (Jun 20, 2025), that Ascend's ecosystem "仍存一定短板，不少用户需要投入大量精力进行开发和维护" (still falls short in places, and many users must put great effort into development and maintenance) [212]; the Xinhuanet app (Apr 17, 2026), of China Mobile's cluster of 384 Ascend cards in Wuhan set "对标H200的单卡优势" (against the H200's single-card edge) [213]; a signed newspaper article carried on Qiushi's site (Jul 23, 2026), that home-made computing trails Nvidia's CUDA in operator-library completeness, distributed parallelism strategies and graphics-memory optimisation, so that "同等功耗下单位Token的生成成本偏高、有效算力转化率偏低" (at the same power each token costs more and less of the computing is put to use) [214]; China Electronics News, as carried by EEFocus (Sept 3, 2026), that hidden costs of moving code and tuning make home-made GPUs' "综合使用成本高于国外方案" (total cost of use higher than the foreign option) [215]; and a commentary on People's Daily Online (Oct 30, 2026), that "高端训练芯片的国产替代进程仍需加速" (domestic substitution for high-end training chips still needs to speed up) [216]. A Chinese maker of AI chips, Enflame, says in its final prospectus of Sept 8, 2026 that international makers led by Nvidia are ahead of home-made ones in hardware, software and systems, "均领先于国产厂商" [217]. Two speakers discuss CUDA compatibility and software barriers for home-made chips: Baidu's cloud president, Dou Shen, at its developer conference of Apr 25, 2025, that Chinese and foreign large models that run on CUDA can also run on the Kunlunxin P800, "迁移成本非常低" (at very low migration cost) [218]; and HiSilicon chief scientist Liao Heng, as quoted by 科创板日报 on Oct 6, that two years before "CUDA在这方面显然具有压倒性优势" (CUDA plainly had the overwhelming edge in that respect) and that now "差距已大幅缩小" (the gap has much narrowed) [219]; Dou speaks of Kunlunxin's P800 and Liao of Huawei's Ascend, with no figures for migration cost or the software gap. Its reply to the exchange's listing inquiry says that at the start of the period its accounts cover, when home-made AI chips were not yet mature, internet companies bought mainly international products for commercial reasons: "出于商业化考量，互联网行业客户采购主要以国际产品为主"; and that after the state's East Data, West Computing hub project began at the end of 2022, computing-centre projects became one of the main early sources of demand for home-made AI chips: "智算中心项目随之成为国产AI芯片初期主要的需求场景之一" [220]. The record now holds fourteen more texts of chip makers and builders of computing, among them: Enflame's chairman, in a September 2026 listing roadshow account carried by Eastmoney, on cards for training large AI models, "该场景由国际厂商产品主导" (international makers' products dominate this use case) [221]; Enflame's reply of June 2026, on a centre project that did not go ahead, with the customer's original plan recorded as "计划部署 80%的英伟达产品和 20%的国产 AI 加速卡" (to deploy 80% Nvidia products and 20% home-made AI cards) [222]; the builder 弘信电子, in June 2026, that it still depends to some extent on makers of high-end computing chips because "算力业务对芯片性能要求较高" (computing work asks much of chip performance) [223]; and Huawei's rotating chairman, Xu Zhijun, as quoted by Jiemian News in October 2026, that the 950DT supernodes for training "目前还在测试中" (are still being tested), and that the key to their future use might be "我们的供货能力有多大" (how much we can supply) [224]. A model maker says the same of its rivals. iFlytek's record of its results meeting of Aug 21, 2026: other model makers commonly use Nvidia chips two generations ahead in performance, with a better ecosystem and more efficient training and porting of algorithms, "其他模型厂商普遍使用性能领先两代的英伟达芯片，其生态更完善，各类算法的训练与移植效率更高". iFlytek itself is on the US Entity List, the same record says [225]: a list of foreign parties that are, in the Commerce Department's words, "subject to specific license requirements for the export, reexport, and transfer (in-country) of specified items" [226]. Its report for the first half of 2026 says home-made computing "对比英伟达中端芯片H200尚存在明显差距" (still falls clearly short of Nvidia's mid-range H200 chip), though it says a model trained and run wholly on it was released on Apr 29 [227]. The record of Aug 21 puts the gap, about fivefold against the H200 in sampling efficiency during training, mainly in training on very long contexts, says of Huawei's 950DT, set against the H200, "根据不同任务，综合性能已达其 65%-85%" (by task, its overall performance has reached 65% to 85% of the H200's), and expects American controls to turn its rivals too: "随着美国对顶尖芯片出口管制越来越严格，未来两年内国内同行同样很难再采购到英伟达高端芯片，全行业都会被倒逼转向国产算力" (as American export controls on top chips grow stricter, its peers in China too will within two years find Nvidia's high-end chips hard to buy, and the whole industry will be forced onto home-made computing) [225]. The record now holds twenty-two more texts of model makers and AI companies on reliance on Nvidia, access to advanced chips and adapting to home-made computing, among them: iFlytek's share-issue proposal (September 2025), that the industry's algorithmic innovations in larger-parameter models, MoE and long-chain-of-thought deep reasoning models "仍然高度依赖英伟达生态" (still depend heavily on Nvidia's ecosystem) [228]; its chairman, Liu Qingfeng, on Oct 22, 2026, that "同一个算法，英伟达卡上一个月验证完，移到国产算力上三个月才到60%、半年才到80%" (an algorithm validated in a month on Nvidia cards reaches 60% in three months and 80% in six on home-made computing) [229]; Baidu's chief executive, Robin Li, to TIME (November 2025, in a Chinese rendering), "我非常希望能够使用最先进的 英伟达 芯片" (I would very much like to use the most advanced Nvidia chips) [230]; and the GLM-5 report (code at zai-org on GitHub), adapting its model to seven Chinese chip platforms: "Adapting GLM-5 to diverse Chinese chip infrastructures presents significant challenges due to the heterogeneity of hardware ecosystems" [231].

**7.** **The orders** (press reports citing unnamed people, then three exchange notices). In the first three months of 2025, Chinese companies including ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent "have placed at least $16 billion in orders for" Nvidia's H20 chips, The Information reported, by two people, as Reuters relayed it [232]. Reuters, Dec 31, 2025, by two people: Chinese technology companies "have placed orders for more than 2 million H200 chips for 2026" [233]. Reuters reported on Oct 20, 2026 that ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent "have ordered more than 3 million of" Nvidia's RTX PRO 5500 cards, priced together at over $35 billion, "in a rush for a less powerful but export-compliant chip", and, by two of its sources, that "Total orders from customers in China have exceeded 6 million" [234]. Bloomberg's own Japanese excerpt, dated Nov 4, 2026, of its report on the same card: Chinese AI and cloud companies, Alibaba, Tencent and ByteDance among them, "合計約600万個発注している" (have ordered about 6 million in all), orders said to come to about $70 billion; and, by people it does not name, "各社が今後4カ月に受け取れると予想しているのは数万個程度で、注文全体の約1%に過ぎない" (what the companies expect to receive in the next four months is some tens of thousands, only about 1% of all the orders), while Nvidia struggles to raise production [235]. Reuters, as carried by The Standard, had reported on Oct 20, by one source, that Nvidia told partners "they might receive only 5 percent to 10 percent of the quantities requested", and by another that Nvidia had indicated it "could produce no more than 3 million RTX PRO 5500 cards globally in 2027" [236]; Bloomberg's English text of Nov 4, as carried by Yahoo Finance, adds, by one person citing a key buyer's internal calculations, that the large Chinese buyers "estimate they could receive roughly 700,000 RTX Pro 5500 units by the end of 2027" [237]. These are different measures, each on unnamed people's word. What Beijing is reported to have done about this card is in point 19. The record now holds six more press reports on chip stocks, purchases, orders and plans to lease or order, each on unnamed people's word or the outlet's own: Nikkei, Apr 23, 2025, drawing on Nikkei Asia's reporting, that Chinese firms appeared to have secured H20 stock worth more than 11 billion dollars before the April US export restriction, "百十数億ドル相当の在庫を確保" [238] (stock secured, not orders: another measure than the $16 billion); Reuters, as The Business Times relays it on Jul 31, 2025, that Nvidia had ordered "300,000 H20 chipsets" from TSMC "due to strong demand" [239]; 36Kr, in its coverage of Huang's July 2025 visit, reporting that Tencent, ByteDance and Alibaba "早在H20被禁前，已经提前扫货了10万颗以上" (had bought up at least 100,000 H20 chips before the ban; the ban the article means is American, "在美方禁令的阴影下") [240]; Commercial Times, as relayed by USTV, citing unnamed supply-chain sources on the urgency to order after Washington cleared H20 sales to China again, "先搶單、能買多少是多少" (grab orders first, buy as many as can be had) [241]; Leiphone, as relayed by NetEase on Oct 31, 2025, that a mid-sized Chinese model maker reportedly planned to lease "60台H200服务器" (60 H200 servers) for a year [242]; and Bloomberg, as relayed by the Taipei Times on Jan 9, 2026, citing one unnamed person, that Alibaba and ByteDance "have privately told Nvidia that they want to order more than 200,000 units each of the H200" [243]. Three listed Chinese companies announced purchases of cards named PRO 5500 to the Shenzhen exchange, one a week before the Reuters report and two after it; of 333 notices dated Sept 14 to Nov 24, 2026 on the disclosure site cninfo.com.cn whose titles name a contract, a purchase or computing, these three alone name the card (tables/rtx5500_notices.tsv). Tianyang Technology, Oct 13, 2026: "20,000 张具体型号为 NVIDIA RTX PRO 5500 Blackwell 的英伟达 GPU 产品" (20,000 Nvidia GPUs of that model), under a contract to buy them from a seller it calls only Company X, and "区别于大模型训练用 GPU 产品" (unlike GPUs for training large models), mainly for inference, graphics and other professional work [244]. The exchange later found that Tianyang's earlier report of the purchase, a post on its WeChat account of Oct 9, did not say what the cards were for, and called it "不准确、不完整" (inaccurate, incomplete) [245]. Youzu, for a wholly owned subsidiary, in a notice dated Nov 3, 2026: GPUs of the model PRO5500 (PCIe) for 4,225 million yuan including tax, a price the notice says may change, from a seller it calls only Company A; the notice does not name the maker [246]. Hangzhou Yuanlin (杭州园林), for a controlled subsidiary, in a notice dated Nov 11, 2026: 20,000 "NVIDIA RTX PRO5500 BLACKWELL 及配件产品" (and accessories) from one named seller and 10,000 from another, with no amount given; the company "暂未锁定下游销售订单" (has not yet locked in downstream sales orders) [247]. All three notices name "海外技术管制" (overseas technology controls) among the risks to delivery, and none names a Chinese approval [244] [246] [247]. By Nov 25, 2026 none of the three had filed a notice that a card was delivered or imported, that a Chinese approval was given, or that its contract had ended: each of their notices on the disclosure site cninfo.com.cn dated Oct 1 to Nov 25, 2026 was read (tables/rtx5500_buyers_later_notices.tsv). Youzu's notice had said "公司将持续跟进合同执行情况并及时履行信息披露义务" (the company will keep following the contract's performance and meet its disclosure obligations in time) [246].

**8.** **The pushing back.** The Financial Times, Aug 20, 2025: Chinese regulators had urged more use of home-made chips in recent years, "but tech giants from Alibaba to ByteDance argued that their AI development would be impaired without Nvidia's chips"; the paper adds that, by some close to the companies, "they have become more accepting of a switch, especially for" inference [248]. A week earlier, on Tencent's results call of Aug 13, 2025, asked about its options for buying chips amid the news of restrictions, its president, Martin Lau, said "we don't really have a definite answer on the import situation yet. I think there's a lot of discussion between the two governments" and "we do have enough chips for training and continuous upgrade of our existing models. We also have many options for inference chips", naming neither Nvidia nor the H20 (Stock Analysis's transcript) [249]. Four more texts the record now holds describe GPU demand, reliance or shortages, with some referring specifically to Nvidia's chips: Tencent's president, explaining on an earnings call why its capital spending of 2025 fell short of plan, as China Securities Journal reports him in June 2026, "我们也想买卡，但在很长一段时间内都面临买不到的情况" (we wanted to buy cards too, but for a long time could not get them) [250]; Nvidia's chief executive at the Consumer Electronics Show in January 2026, as Investing.com paraphrases him in a report carried by Yahoo Finance, on high demand from China for the H200 [251]; and, before the years this note covers, a Baidu employee to the Financial Times in 2023, as Data Center Dynamics relays it, "Without these Nvidia chips, we can’t pursue the training for any large language model" [252], and the academician Zheng Weimin, whose warning of late 2023 a commentary site quotes, "国外一卡（GPU）难求，国产卡使用意愿低" (foreign GPUs hard to get, little will to use home-made cards) [253]. None of the four remarks names a Chinese rule. Reuters, Mar 27, 2026, by people it did not name: "Despite a government campaign to encourage the use of domestic semiconductors", Huawei had "struggled to persuade big tech firms in the private sector to adopt its current flagship chip, the Ascend 910C, in large quantities"; ByteDance and Alibaba now planned to order its new Ascend 950PR after customer tests went well, "much happier now that the chip is more compatible with Nvidia's Cuda software system and has better response speeds" [254] [255]. The companies described chip sales, use of domestic computing and plans for more cloud capacity: Enflame told the Shanghai exchange in June 2026 that during the reporting period Tencent's demand for AI accelerator cards "远超公司目前供应能力" (far exceeds what the company can supply), its direct sales to Tencent Technology (Shenzhen) and corresponding AVAP sales together coming to 83.79% of its operating revenue in the last period reported [256]; Tencent's chief strategy officer said on its call of May 13, 2026, in Time Finance's Chinese account carried by Sina, "随着中国设计的GPU供应逐步增加，我们将弥补这一情况，向腾讯云提供更多容量" (as the supply of China-designed GPUs grows, we will make up for it and give Tencent Cloud more capacity) [257]; and Volcano Engine president Tan Dai said in an interview after Volcano Engine's June 23, 2026 conference, as reported by Jiemian News, "关于国产算力，我们用得非常多" (as for home-made computing, we use a great deal of it), a share he did not give [258]. Reuters's headline of Sept 4, 2025: "Chinese firms still want Nvidia chips despite government pressure not to buy, sources say" [259]. Nori Chiou, an investment director at White Oak Capital Partners, quoted by Reuters in December 2025, said cloud companies were "lobbying the government to relax restrictions on a conditional basis" [260]. The founder of 360, Zhou Hongyi, asked it in public, on TMTPost's video podcast 《DeepPractice深度实践》, in the transcript on TMTPost's own site (a copy on another site is dated Oct 14, 2026): "所以美国现在实际上允许卖H200给我们，但中国还在限制购买，可以适度开放一下" (so America in fact now allows the H200 to be sold to us, but China still restricts buying; it could open up somewhat) [261] [262]; it is his account, and he names no rule or body (his words on training are in point 20). On Jul 30, 2026 a company asked the commerce ministry, on its public message board, how to import H200 chips lawfully: "我司计划合规进口H200数据中心GPU" [263]. The ministry's reply of Aug 19, 2026 speaks only of exporters and export controls; it does not answer the question about imports. Its text is, word for word, the reply its Security and Control Bureau gave on Jul 3, 2026 to a company asking whether photodiodes it wanted to export were dual-use items: "我司拟出口光电二极管" (our company plans to export photodiodes) [264]. A Chinese trade site, Leiphone, Aug 31, 2025, quoted a salesman it did not name, who was still upbeat about the H20 market: "国内还是有需求的，但至于现在能不能采，什么时候采就不太清楚" (there is still demand at home, but whether one can buy now, and when, is not very clear). It also quoted a manager who had worked closely with Nvidia, under a name the site says is a pseudonym, Zheng Xing: there was hardly any demand for the H20, state firms and other government-led buyers would have none, and "BAT也在响应国家号召，不会再买新的H20" (Baidu, Alibaba and Tencent, too, are answering the state's call and will not buy new H20s) [265]. Hanna Dohmen, a research analyst at Georgetown University's Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET), put the general point in her prepared statement for a hearing of the US-China commission, Feb 6, 2025: before the American controls "the Chinese government fought against the strong pull of commercial incentives", and "Chinese consumers preferred to use the highest-performance chips designed by Nvidia and other foreign firms" [266].

**9.** **They reportedly went abroad for Nvidia chips, and one lab was reported to go back to them for training; other claims name home-made chips.** Alibaba and ByteDance "are among the tech groups training their latest large language models (LLMs) in data centres across Southeast Asia", by two people the Financial Times cites (November 2025); by the same people, "there had been a steady increase in training in offshore locations after the Trump administration moved in April to restrict sales of the H20" [267]. Tencent's president spoke of such capacity on its results call of Nov 18, 2026, asked about the latest American restrictions: "everyone is actually having less compute, advanced compute to work with, no matter when you actually started leasing or building capacities outside of China" [268]; a Chinese report of the call gives the answer to him, 刘炽平, in reported speech: "无论租赁还是自建算力都面临约束" (leased or self-built, computing faces constraints) [269]. The leasing the paper describes is, by its account, "compliant with US export controls" [267]. Shipping chips to such companies' own names abroad is another matter: in the licence table of May 11, 2026 (point 16), none of the five applications to ship Nvidia chips to an ultimate location in Malaysia, Indonesia or Thailand, for consignees such as Bytedance System and Alibaba Cloud, was approved (1,118,500 chips; the sum is mine): all are marked RWA [5], the short form of the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security for "Returned without action (RWA)" [270]; the papers do not say why. The bureau's rule of Jan 15, 2026, which moved exports of some advanced chips to end users in China and Macau to case-by-case review, kept a presumption of denial for "exports to entities that are headquartered or have a parent company headquartered in Macau or a destination in Country Group D:5, including end-users located outside of destinations in Country Group D:5 or Macau" [271], and the director of the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy, Michael Kratsios, told a House subcommittee on Jan 14, 2026: "if you're a large Chinese tech company and you want to build a data center in Malaysia, you cannot buy H200s to do that. You can only import those H200s for facilities in China." [272]; neither speaks of any one application. The bureau's rule published on Nov 19, 2026 sets, first, restrictions on exports "to overseas data centers that remotely supply significant volumes of compute to companies headquartered in Country Group D:5 or Macau", a group that includes China, which "will undermine the ability of companies headquartered in countries of concern from using U.S. advanced Super Intelligence (SI) semiconductors to train advanced SI models": a licence is needed where a data centre gives such companies access to more than 12,000,000 "aggregate TPP", "unless the computing power is exclusively used for commercial recommender systems"; its preamble says an earlier control already reached exports made on behalf of such a company, as when it "was the exclusive or primary (greater than 50 percent of total compute) remote end user of the data center computing capacity". It adds a licence requirement for advanced chips bound for Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand or Brazil, "countries which present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion", except for companies headquartered, or whose ultimate parent company is headquartered, in the United States, "provided certain reporting requirements are met"; it does not speak of the returned applications [273]. Malaysia's trade minister, whose government was seeking clarification of new American restrictions on AI chips, told Malaysian journalists on Nov 18, 2026 that whoever wants American GPUs for a data centre must comply with the US rules, and gave data-centre operators this advice: "If you are not prepared to comply, then you should not operate in Malaysia" [274]. Two more official texts and a press report quoting a US official bear on computing abroad: the Bureau of Industry and Security's policy statement of May 13, 2025 says a licence may be needed to export, reexport or transfer in-country to data centres advanced computing chips and commodities subject to the EAR when the exporter, reexporter or transferor has “knowledge” that the SI model will be used for a WMD or military-intelligence end use or end user and that the IaaS provider will use the items to train SI models for or on behalf of parties headquartered in D:5 countries (including China) or Macau [275]; Malaysia's trade ministry had said on Jul 14, 2025 that "effective immediately, all exports, tranships and transits of high-performance AI chips of US origin are subject to a Strategic Trade Permit" [276]; and the director of the White House science office wrote of Moonshot on X on Jul 22, 2026 that it "has also acquired GB300-equipped servers and has accessed GB300s in Thailand, likely to train its AI models." [277], his account, with the training purpose described as likely; Bloomberg reported the post the next day [278]. Reuters, Oct 28, 2026, as Euronext carries it: the commerce ministry's draft of October 2026 (point 17), in the analysis of the law firm Han Kun, "provides simplified approvals for Chinese companies building and operating their own facilities abroad, but not expressly for transferring technology to foreign customers to build equivalent systems"; and, by a person briefed on the matter, "Researchers at major Chinese AI labs are seeking clarification on whether they can train or run their most advanced models on foreign-owned overseas servers" [279]. The Financial Times reported on Dec 20, 2025, by people it cites, that Tencent had the use of advanced Nvidia chips owned by a Japanese firm, Datasection, in a data centre outside Osaka; the firm would not confirm its customer, and Tencent said its use of "cloud computing services is both transparent and legal". The paper calls this "a legal but geopolitically fraught strategy" and gives American curbs as the cause: "China's big tech companies have been forced by US curbs to seek ways of accessing Nvidia's top-tier chips overseas" [280]. Datasection's own filings name their customer only as "世界最大規模のクラウドサービスプロバイダー" (one of the world's largest cloud service providers), for a centre to be opened in Osaka [281]; in January 2026 they put the start of that service back to March 2026, the customer having been served since September 2025 on a partner's GPU servers [282], and a notice of May 7, 2026 says the centre had been moved from Osaka to Inzai, in Chiba [283]. Datasection's notices of Nov 16, 2026 say that full operation of its centre in Japan and of two abroad, expected in stages from July 2026, had slipped, "フル稼働の開始見込時期に遅れが生じておりました" (the expected start of full operation had been delayed); that the owner of a centre abroad had given notice ending its lease after talks with its customer on changing from B200 to B300 servers dragged on; and they cut the sales forecast for the year to March 2027 from 162,193 to 17,286 million yen [284] [285]. DeepSeek "delayed the release of its new model after failing to train it using Huawei's chips": by three unnamed people it "was encouraged by authorities to adopt Huawei's Ascend processor rather than use Nvidia's systems", met "persistent technical issues", and was prompted to use "Nvidia chips for training and Huawei's for inference", while, by people the paper cites, it "was still working with Huawei to make the model compatible with Ascend for inference" (Financial Times, Aug 13, 2025) [286]. Another unnamed person gave the paper a second cause of the delay, slow labelling of data.
