DeepSeek's own papers name Nvidia's chips for its earlier models, with counts and hours: V3 "is trained on a cluster equipped with 2048 NVIDIA H800 GPUs", in 2.788 million GPU hours, by its technical report of Dec 27, 2024 [287] [288], and R1-Zero and R1 on "64*8 H800 GPUs", in about 198 and 80 hours, by the second version of its R1 paper, of Jan 4, 2026 [289]. A smaller lab's reports say the same of its models: "Kimi K2.5 is trained on NVIDIA H800 GPU clusters" [290], as slides of Yang Zhilin, named on them as Kimi's founder and chief executive, put it for K2 and K2.5 both [291]; StepFun's paper works out the theoretical decoding cost of its Step-3 on Nvidia's chips, "(with AFD, H800 and H20)" [292], and its chief executive, asked "未来是否考虑全面“去英伟达化”？" (would it consider dropping Nvidia entirely in future?), answered that which chip it uses "不是主观选择，而是现实条件决定的" (is not a subjective choice but set by real conditions) [293]. The record now holds 15 more papers and pages in which Chinese model makers name Nvidia chips for training or tuning; among them the February 2026 Step 3.5 Flash preprint: "Step 3.5 Flash is trained on a large-scale cluster with 4,096 NVIDIA H800 GPUs." [294]; MiniMax's on MiniMax-01, of January 2025, "the number of H800 GPUs ranges from 1500 to 2500" [295], and on MiniMax-M1, of June 2025, "full RL training on 512 H800 GPUs to complete in only three weeks" [296]; ByteDance's researchers, of two pretraining jobs of its production-grade models: "These pretraining jobs were run on a GPU cluster comprising 9,600 Hopper GPUs." [297]; the Tessera paper by Alibaba Cloud and Huazhong University of Science and Technology researchers, on production pre-training of the Qwen3 and Qwen3-Next model families at a scale beyond "10,000 NVIDIA Hopper GPUs" [298]; and Kai-Fu Lee, writing about Yi-Lightning on LinkedIn in October 2024: "it was trained on 2000 H100s for 1 month" [299]; the MiniMax-M1 paper reports a rental cost of $534,700 for its reinforcement-learning run. Later claims run the other way, each the model maker's, the chip maker's or a local government's own; of the fourteen later claims of training on home-made chips cited here, seven give the size of the cluster that did the training, and three come with a measure of time: the post-training of V4-Pro, in the account of the institute whose team did it, and two of those cited last, Peng Cheng Laboratory's and Baidu's (below). Zhipu said in January 2026 that its image model had completed "its full training cycle on Chinese-made chips", Huawei's Ascend; the same report adds: "Zhipu did not say how many processors or how long it took to train its model" [300]. Zhipu's own page heads the claim "国产芯片完成全程训练的SOTA模型" (a state-of-the-art model trained throughout on home-made chips) and names the hardware for the model's autoregressive base: "模型自回归结构基座基于昇腾Atlas 800T A2设备与昇思MindSpore AI框架" (the autoregressive base, on Ascend Atlas 800T A2 devices and the MindSpore framework) [301]. Meituan's technical blog calls its model of June 2026 "业界首个在五万卡国产算力集群上完成全流程训练与推理的万亿参数模型" (the industry's first trillion-parameter model to complete the whole course of training and inference on a home-made computing cluster of 50,000 cards) [302]. Of DeepSeek's later model V4, Reuters wrote in April 2026: "Huawei said its chips were used in some of the V4's training process" [303]. Huawei Cloud's page of that day speaks of adapting and serving the model, and a post of that day signed 昇腾CANN (Ascend CANN) on the AtomGit open-source site, of a training reference implementation offered "为便于用户快速微调" (so that users can fine-tune quickly) [304] [305]; neither says that Huawei's chips trained V4. China Daily reported that day that "DeepSeek added that the V4 model has already completed inference adaptation on Huawei's Ascend platform" [306]. DeepSeek's own report on V4 names no chip for its training, and says one of its schemes was validated "on both NVIDIA GPUs and HUAWEI Ascend NPUs platforms" [307]. A rival, iFlytek, told investors days later that "DS V4 的训练仍然是基于英伟达卡进行的" (V4's training was still done on Nvidia cards), a competitor's claim that nobody has checked [308]. In June 2026 a group that includes Huawei said, by the Shenzhen government's account as the trade site Tom's Hardware relays it, that it had completed "full-parameter post-training" of V4-Pro on a cluster of "at least 1,000 Huawei Ascend 910C chips"; the site's own caution: "it doesn't demonstrate that the chips can pre-train a frontier model from scratch", and "DeepSeek itself hasn't commented" [309]. The Shenzhen Loop Area Institute, whose team did the work with units of Huawei and others, says in its own announcement, dated May 28, 2026, that it was done "within just one month" and that "our team ran a 1.6T MoE model on a thousand-card cluster at a steady pace of 27 seconds per step for over 1,500 consecutive steps"; the work it describes is continual pre-training and fine-tuning of the released model, and its list of results gives the 1,500 steps under "Sustained SFT training" [310], the fine-tuning. China Telecom's Xingchen AGI Lab wrote in November 2026 that a 285-billion-parameter model of its Xing 4 series "is pretrained on 8,192 Ascend 910C cards" [311]. China Telecom had made such a claim two years before, outside the fourteen: its AI institute's repository dates to Sept 20, 2024 the release of TeleChat2-115B, "首个完全国产算力训练并开源的千亿参数模型" (the first open model of 100 billion parameters trained wholly on home-made computing) [312], and its report of July 2025 says the TeleChat2, TeleChat2.5 and T1 series "were trained on the Atlas 900 A2 cluster, powered by 8,000 Ascend NPUs", where the first TeleChat "was trained on a cluster with 640 NVIDIA A100 GPUs" [313]. Three more claims of 2026 name no cluster that did the training. iFlytek's chairman released on Oct 22, 2026 what People's Daily Overseas Edition calls "基于全国产算力训练的端云一体星火X2.6大模型" (the Spark X2.6 large model, device and cloud in one, trained on wholly home-made computing), and the paper quotes him: "只有真正地在国产算力上训练出自己的模型，才是真的自主可控。" (only by really training one's own model on home-made computing does one have true independent control) [314]; it adds that a home-made cluster of ten thousand cards supports the model, "支撑这套模型的，是科大讯飞已上线的万卡国产服务集群" [314], and does not say that this cluster trained it. The chairman's own words after the forum, as reported by Blue Whale News and carried by Sina Finance on Oct 23, are of part of the training: "讯飞星火X2.6也是首次在国产算力上完成超长上下文代码与智能体任务的训练" (Spark X2.6 also completed training for very long-context code and agent tasks on home-made computing for the first time) [315]. Meituan's technical team wrote on Nov 10, 2026 of its model LongCat-2.5: "在单卡显存不足 80 GB 的国产 AI 加速器上实现了长序列训练与微调" (long-sequence training and fine-tuning were done on home-made AI accelerators with under 80 GB of memory a card) [316]; it names no chip maker. Meituan's own release of the model, which dates it Nov 2, says more: "训练全程由国产算力完成" (its training was done throughout on home-made computing) [317]. And a page of OpenBMB is titled "面壁智能联合清华正式开源中国首个基于昇腾训练的 1.58-bit 端侧大模型 BitCPM-CANN" (ModelBest, with Tsinghua, formally open-sources BitCPM-CANN, China's first 1.58-bit on-device large model trained on Ascend) [318]; the page, dated May 25, 2026, names no cluster. The record now holds 21 more claims, in model makers', chip makers' and laboratories' own papers and pages, of training or tuning on home-made chips or with home-made computing power; among them the Pangu Ultra paper, of April 2025, before the later claims: "when training Pangu Ultra on 8,192 Ascend NPUs" [319]; the Qianfan-VL paper, of September 2025: "All models are trained entirely on Baidu’s Kunlun P800 chips" [320], with a reported capability of "over 90% scaling efficiency on 5000+ chips for a single task" [320], and the Qianfan-OCR paper, of March 2026: "All training is conducted on 1,024 Baidu Kunlun P800 chips" [321], "The full four-stage pipeline completes within a week" [321]; Peng Cheng Laboratory's page with 2026-01-04 in its URL, on a 2-billion-parameter model made with Tsinghua University: "1024张国产算力芯片集群长达12.5天高强度训练" (twelve and a half days of intensive training on a cluster of 1,024 home-made chips) [322]; Moore Threads, the chip maker, in the investor relations record of its results meeting of Sept 3, 2026, of an unnamed partner: "从零起步完整训练 MoE-236B 超大参数基础大模型" (trained a MoE-236B base model completely, from scratch) on "MTT S5000 万卡级智算集群" (an MTT S5000 computing cluster of the ten-thousand-card class) [323]; and the Intern Lumina U2 paper, of October 2026: "On the Ascend platform, we use 1,024 Ascend 910B NPUs in all stages; on the NVIDIA platform, we use 192 GPUs in all stages." [324], the Ascend run being its main model; and China Telecom's AI institute, in the repository of TeleChat3, which dates the open release of TeleChat3-105B-A4.7B-Thinking and TeleChat3-36B-Thinking to Dec 23, 2025: "星辰语义大模型 TeleChat3 是由中国电信人工智能研究院研发训练的大语言模型，该系列模型 完全基于国产算力 训练。" (TeleChat3 is a large language model developed and trained by China Telecom's AI institute, and the models in the series were trained wholly with home-made computing power) [325], which names no cluster.

## 3. What Beijing did about it

Points 10 to 12 rest on documents, filings and named officials; points 13 to 16 on press reports citing unnamed people. Point 16 also sets Washington's own table of licence applications, a document, beside those reports.

### On the record

**10.** **Regulators acted against Nvidia by name.** The market regulator announced on Dec 9, 2024 that it had opened an antitrust case: "市场监管总局依法对英伟达公司开展立案调查" [326]. On Jul 31, 2025 the cyberspace regulator summoned Nvidia, "约谈了英伟达公司", over the security risks of vulnerabilities and backdoors in the H20 chips it sells in China [327]. On Sept 15, 2025 the market regulator announced a preliminary finding against Nvidia, and a further investigation: "市场监管总局依法决定对其实施进一步调查" [328]. That notice cites the antimonopoly law and the regulator's 2020 conditions on Nvidia's purchase of Mellanox, and gives no facts. Nvidia's own account of the finding points the other way: "our compliance with applicable U.S. export controls, which required us to offer degraded products to the Chinese market, discriminated unfairly against customers in the China market" [329]. By that account the case faults Nvidia for offering Chinese customers degraded products, not for selling to them. An unsigned article on the work of the regulator's Party group, in the Party members' paper of Sept 24, 2026, counts the case as political work: "依法对英伟达、高通公司、杜邦中国集团立案调查，在重要时间节点上发布权威声音，有力维护国家利益" (it opened formal investigations into Nvidia, Qualcomm and DuPont China Group under the law, spoke with authority at important moments, and forcefully safeguarded national interests) [330]. Xinhua, in March 2026, had named the further investigation of Nvidia among the regulator's enforcement steps, and said the series of measures sends a clear signal of treating all kinds of companies alike: "坚持对各类企业一视同仁" [331]. One American body read the case as a lever in trade talks. The US-China commission's report of November 2025, citing Reuters and the Financial Times, says the finding came "in the midst of negotiations", as the regulator dropped an inquiry into Google, "signaling China's willingness to both exert leverage and ease pressure as a negotiating tactic" [332]. The White House said on Nov 1, 2025 that "China will terminate its various investigations targeting U.S. companies in the semiconductor supply chain, including its antitrust, anti-monopoly, and anti-dumping investigations" [333]; the commerce ministry's account of the deal names none of China's investigations of American firms and, of such companies, says only "相关企业个案处理" (handling the individual cases of the companies concerned) [334]. The Trade Representative's report of March 2026 says that by the end of 2025 China "had terminated or suspended these investigations", which "were being pursued for retaliatory purposes"; it names no company [335]. No page read says the Nvidia case ended: the regulator's review of its 2025 work, posted Apr 10, 2026, still lists "对英伟达公司实施进一步调查" [336], and its annual report on antitrust enforcement for 2025, posted Jun 5, 2026, lists the further investigation with no outcome [337] [338]; its news list to Nov 23, 2026 has a suspension of the case against DuPont China and none for Nvidia [339] [340], and its antitrust bureau's list of work news, read on Nov 24, 2026, has nothing on Nvidia after the notice of Sept 15, 2025 [341]; and Nvidia's report of August 2026 still lists the finding among its risks [329].

**11.** **Companies told securities regulators that the state restricted purchases.** Nvidia told the Securities and Exchange Commission on Nov 19, 2025 that the Chinese government "restricted customers from purchasing, importing, or using our data center products" [4]. From November 2025 on, Nvidia's reports to the Securities and Exchange Commission also put its being in effect shut out of China's data-centre market down to American export controls and to needing both governments' approval [4]. In the report of August 2026 the sentence on the H200 restriction is followed at once by a $0.4 billion charge "as the demand for H200 products diminished"; no cause is given for the fall [329]. On the results calls held the same days, Nvidia named no Chinese restriction. Aug 27, 2025: "We have not included H20 in our Q3 outlook as we continue to work through geopolitical issues" [342]. May 20, 2026: "we have yet to generate any revenue, and we are uncertain whether any imports will be allowed into the country" [343]. Aug 26, 2026, the day of the report that says "restricted by the PRC government": "given ongoing geopolitical uncertainty, there is no China data center compute revenue in our forward outlook" [344]. The table below gives Nvidia's words to the Securities and Exchange Commission, report by report.

| Filed | Nvidia's words on the Chinese government | Beside them, in the same report |
|---|---|---|
| Nov 20, 2024, 10-Q [345] | it "may also encourage customers to purchase from our China-based competitors, or impose restrictions on the sale to certain customers of our products" | neither of the sentences quoted in the rows below |
| Aug 27, 2025, 10-Q [346] [347] | it "has and may continue to encourage customers to purchase from our China-based competitors, or impose restrictions on the sale to certain customers of our products"; "China's government publicly questioned whether our H20 products have built-in vulnerabilities, discouraging customers from purchasing our products" | the questioning came "following U.S. legislative proposals calling for mandatory features in our chips"; Nvidia "may be unable to create a competitive product for China's data center market that receives approval from the USG" |
| Nov 19, 2025, 10-Q [4] | it "has encouraged customers to purchase from our China-based competitors and restricted customers from purchasing, importing, or using our data center products, including any China-specific product designed to comply with U.S. export controls" | "we are already effectively foreclosed from the China market by U.S. export controls"; Nvidia is "unable to create and deliver a competitive product for China's data center market that receives approval from both the USG and the Chinese government" |
| Feb 25 and May 20, 2026, 10-K and 10-Q [348] [349] | the same, with "discouraged customers from purchasing, importing, or using our data center products" in place of "restricted"; of the H200 licences, it does "not yet know whether any imports will be allowed into China" | both kept |
| Aug 26, 2026, 10-Q [329] | "discouraged" kept; sales under the American licences for the H20 and the H200 "were restricted by the PRC government" | both kept; the second now reads "for wide distribution in China's data center market with the approval from both the USG and the Chinese government" |

Of the latest annual reports of fifteen US-listed Chinese companies read for this note, only Alibaba's says a Chinese authority asked Chinese technology companies to stop buying certain chips: "In September 2025, the Cyberspace Administration of China requested Chinese technology companies to halt purchases of certain chips." [350] It does not name Nvidia. It follows the words "On the other hand" and a sentence in which the regulator, in May 2023, "banned the use of its products from key infrastructure projects in China", the products of an American memory-chip maker [350]; the published review conclusion, on the regulator's site, told operators of critical information infrastructure to stop buying them, "应停止采购美光公司产品" (shall stop buying Micron's products) [351] (point 18). Both sentences stand in a passage on American export controls, and the next sentence says such restrictions "have affected and may have further material adverse effects" on Alibaba's access to technology and computing [350]. Alibaba's Chinese annual report for the same year, dated May 20, 2026, gives its risk factors only in summary, "風險因素摘要載列如下" (a summary of the risk factors is set out below), and carries neither sentence; its note on export controls gives that passage's general sentences without its examples [352]. A search of the other fourteen found no such sentence (the same search finds Alibaba's); where they speak of chip supply they name export controls, as Baidu's does: "如果我們因出口管制而無法採購足夠數量的先進半導體芯片" (if export controls leave us unable to buy enough advanced chips) [353]. Two Chinese makers of AI chips, answering the Shanghai exchange's listing inquiries in August and September 2025, explained the H20's prospects by the cyberspace regulator's summons and, Moore Threads, by "美国出口管制政策的不停变化、市场对于其芯片是否存在安全问题的担忧" (the constant changes in US export-control policy, and the market's worry whether its chips are safe) [354], or, MetaX, by "我国对高端算力芯片安全可控的战略方针" (the country's strategic line that high-end computing chips be secure and controllable), adding that national public AI computing platforms have explicit requirements to deploy home-made computing [355]; neither says that an authority told buyers to stop or to justify their orders, and both say their own chips can take the H20's place. Qualcomm says some of its customers in China, makers of phones and other devices, have developed their own chips or use rivals' in place of its own, and that others may, for reasons among which it lists "pressure, encouragement or incentives from, or policies of, the Chinese government" and concerns over losing access "as a result of actual, threatened or potential U.S. or Chinese government actions" (annual report, Nov 5, 2025) [356]. AMD, May 6, 2026, of the chips Washington licensed, which its filing calls MI325 products: "We do not yet know whether any imports of MI325 products will be allowed into China" [357]; the sentence is gone from its report of Nov 4, 2026, with no reason given [358]. On results calls the Chinese companies said less: in the 76 results-call transcripts of eight Chinese companies, November 2023 to November 2026, and 42 records of four smaller model makers read for this note, no speaker and no record names a Chinese authority as restricting chip purchases, though 27 of the transcripts leave out passages spoken in another language (an earlier count of 25 read the pages' plain text, which drops a paragraph that holds only the marker); in Chinese records of 25 of those 27 calls (14 of them call transcripts that keep the Chinese passages; none was found for Kingsoft Cloud's calls of May 28 and Aug 20, 2025) no speaker names a Chinese authority beside a chip word either. In the transcript of the stock-data site Stock Analysis, the speaker it labels as Tencent's chief strategy officer, May 13, 2026, put a shortage of chips down to "a combination of policy restrictions on certain, you know, foreign design GPUs being brought into China" and to limited factory capacity for Chinese designs [359]. The answer does not say whose policy. Yicai's Chinese report of the same answer names no speaker and carries only the part about China's own chips, "国内的GPU和ASIC算力一直很紧张，但目前这个问题正得到逐步解决，国内的晶圆厂正在对产能进行补充。" (GPU and ASIC compute in China has been tight, but the problem is now gradually being solved, and China's fabs are adding capacity), and that the company had met no artificial additional limits on CPU and network chips [360]. Sina Tech's Chinese record of the call's questions and answers carries the import half as well, under the same speaker name: "一方面是对部分海外生产、设计的GPU有政策性限制，这部分GPU无法进入中国市场" (on the one hand, policy restrictions on some GPUs produced and designed abroad, which cannot enter the Chinese market) [361]. Another Chinese transcript, on Xueqiu, puts the same answer under the chief financial officer, "发言人：罗硕瀚" [362]; Tencent's own replay was not reachable, so who spoke is not settled.

**12.** **American officials, on the record.** The Commerce Secretary, Howard Lutnick, to a Senate subcommittee, Apr 22, 2026 [363]: "my understanding is their cloud companies want to buy these chips", and the "Chinese central government has not let them as of yet buy the chips because they're trying to keep their investment focused on their own domestic industry" [364]. The US Trade Representative, Jamieson Greer, May 26, 2026: "my own sense is that what passes for the private sector in China is interested in the highest technology chips they can get. And the government in China and the party are interested in promoting their domestic production of chips and indigenous innovation."; "And you can guess who wins in that situation." [365] On Nov 20, 2026, in a Bloomberg interview (machine captions, not checked by ear; 1:06 to 1:35), he said the Chinese side is "not as monolithic as people would have you believe": the private sector is "quite eager to get the most advanced chips they can"; by his "own analysis" some "think, well, we'd like to have access", others "we don't want American chips anyway" and "We want to see the ascend chips expand their market" [366]. A second caption track differs on one word and on a sentence break, so whether he puts the split inside the government needs a listen [367]. The President, Donald Trump, May 15, 2026, asked whether advanced chips such as the H200 had come up with Xi, began "It didn't come up, but, as you know, Jensen was there." and went on, of the H200, "China needs it. And so, yes, it came up."; asked "But they haven't bought any so far?", he said: "No, because they chose not to. They want to try and develop their own. But it did come up, and I think something could happen on that." [368] On Oct 29, 2025, when, after a question about exports of "Nvidia Blackwells or downgraded chips", a reporter put it to him that China had discouraged purchases of those chips, he said: "Well, China actually terminated the relationship with the Nvidia, yes." [369] The next day he said "We did discuss chips", and "they're going to be talking to Nvidia and others about taking chips", and that he had said it was "really between you and Nvidia", with Washington, in his words, "sort of the arbitrator"; asked whether that meant downgraded Blackwell chips, he said "No, no, not the Blackwell.", and the Trade Representative, speaking beside him, added: "The Blackwell chips, of course, weren't discussed." [370] The Treasury Secretary, Scott Bessent, Nov 20, 2026: "I think what we have seen is a paucity of orders from the Chinese on the H200s as they try to build out their own chip stack" [371]. The House Select Committee on China has said both things, each time citing a news report. In a report it released on Apr 16, 2026: "Beijing subsequently approved H200 purchases by Alibaba, Tencent, and ByteDance", citing Reuters [372] [373]. On Nov 13, 2026: "the PRC government refused the H200 chips, instead pushing its own homegrown industry", citing an article on the President's words of May 15 [374] [375]. The Commerce Secretary, the Treasury Secretary and the Trade Representative each give theirs as their own understanding or view. The Commerce Secretary and the Trade Representative set the companies apart from the government; the President and the Treasury Secretary do not. And Senator Chris Coons challenged the Commerce Secretary's account. His letter of May 5, 2026 says Nvidia's chief executive, Jensen Huang, had told reporters that March that Nvidia "had received necessary approvals from both the United States and PRC governments", and: "Your statements before the committee appear to contradict Huang's comments." [376]

### Reported, from unnamed people

**13.** **Guidance from 2024.** The Information reported on May 13, 2024, by two people who work with the companies, that officials had told ByteDance, Tencent, Alibaba and Baidu "to cut back on their purchase of foreign-made artificial intelligence chips in favor of buying more domestically made chips", and that "the Chinese government now wants tech companies to buy equal numbers of domestic and foreign-made AI chips for new internet data centers"; by three people in the talks, firms ordering more foreign chips "will have to detail in writing how many U.S. chips they have ordered for deployment and justify the decision"; the article adds, in its own words, "The new guidance hasn’t yet been strictly enforced" [377] (the article as a publisher's awards file reproduces it; the paper's own page is behind its wall). Reuters, May 24, 2024, in a report that cites three unnamed people in the supply chain, says in its own words: "Another major stumbling block to the success of Nvidia's H20 chip in China has been a directive by Beijing for companies to buy Chinese chips, although two of the three sources said those orders had eased in recent months." By the same report's three sources, the H20 was in "abundant supply", and by two of them it sold "in some cases at an over 10% discount to Huawei's Ascend 910B"; in government procurement data, which it calls "not exhaustive", "just five state or state-affiliated buyers" had shown interest in buying the H20 over six months, "compared with over a dozen for Huawei's 910B"; and, by two of the sources, Alibaba had ordered "over 30,000 H20 chips" [378]. Bloomberg, Sept 27, 2024 (carried by The Business Times on Sept 28, Singapore time), by people it did not name: "Chinese regulators have been discouraging companies from purchasing Nvidia's H20 chips" [379]. The same report gives, by people familiar with China's AI policy, a limit to that guidance: officials also wanted companies to "build the best AI systems possible", and "If that means they need to buy some foreign semiconductors over domestic alternatives, Beijing will still tolerate that"; and, by one of its people, some companies were "turning a blind eye to the Chinese decree to avoid H20 chips and rushing to buy more of them" ahead of an expected American sanction, while also "buying homemade Huawei chips to please Beijing" [379].

**14.** **The summer of 2025.** The earliest press reports of the summer's instructions among those saved for this project came on Aug 12, 2025. Bloomberg, by people it did not name: "Over the past few weeks, Chinese authorities have sent notices to a range of firms discouraging use of the less-advanced semiconductors"; the guidance "was particularly strong against the use of H20s for any government or national security-related work"; and, in Bloomberg's own words, "Beijing has stopped short of outright banning the hardware" [380]. The Financial Times, the same day, by three people: regulators such as the industry ministry had asked companies "to explain why they need to order Nvidia's H20 chips instead of using domestic alternatives", and, by two of the three, some of the main buyers "were planning to downsize their orders"; a data-centre operator said: "It's not banned but has kind of become a politically incorrect thing to do" [381]. That article is held only as a third-party mirror of the copy Ars Technica carried, not from the paper's own site. Reuters, in a copy carried the next day, by three people: the companies "have not been ordered to stop buying H20 chips" [382]. The same copy relays a report by The Information, which Reuters said it "could not immediately confirm", that ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent "had been ordered by the CAC in the past two weeks to suspend Nvidia chip purchases altogether" [382]. Two reports give the stop as lasting until a security check: The Epoch Times (Hong Kong), Aug 13, 2025, in a compiled Chinese report that gives The Information's report first, says that, by two people, the regulators had said purchases of new Nvidia chips must be suspended until their security risks were investigated, "在調查輝達向中國市場銷售的晶片的潛在安全風險之前，必須暫停採購輝達新的晶片" (until the potential security risks of the chips Nvidia sells to the Chinese market are investigated, purchases of Nvidia's new chips must be suspended) [383]; and The Wall Street Journal, Aug 23, 2025, as Mint carries it, says in its own words of the H20: "The government instructed Chinese companies not to buy them until the all-clear was given." [384] The Financial Times named three bodies on Aug 20, 2025, citing people "with knowledge of the regulatory action": "the Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC), the National Development and Reform Commission (NDRC) and the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology (MIIT) moved in response to comments made by Lutnick last month" [248]. Howard Lutnick is the American Commerce Secretary; he had said on television that China was not sold America's "best stuff", and by the paper's sources some Chinese leaders found that "insulting" [248]. About a week after those remarks of Jul 15, by the paper's account, the cyberspace regulator issued what the paper calls "window guidance", "an informal notice", "instructing them to halt new orders for Nvidia's H20 chips"; the industry ministry "also spoke with Chinese tech executives informally to reinforce CAC's stance"; and the state planner asked them to refrain from "purchasing all Nvidia chips, including the H20" [248]. "As a result, Chinese tech groups held off or significantly downsized their H20 orders", the paper wrote, "according to those with knowledge of their plans" [248]. The same article gives a second reason: "Some Chinese tech companies have held off H20 orders because they want to see if the China-specific Blackwell chip, which potentially has better performance, would become available" [248]. The Wall Street Journal, Jul 23, 2026, looking back: "Authorities privately told companies to halt their Nvidia purchases, while officials publicly raised cybersecurity concerns about the U.S. company's products." By people familiar with the message, Vice Premier Ding Xuexiang warned China's largest AI users, in the paper's words: "Anyone who resisted employing domestic chips was a traitor." And of January 2026: "Beijing told some companies they could buy the H200s only when necessary, while demanding a commitment to use more domestic chips." [385] In public, Ding paired self-reliance with openness: at the Digital China Summit on Apr 29, 2026, by Xinhua's report, he said "我國人工智能隻有走自主創新發展之路，才有應對任何遏制打壓的底氣" (only by taking the road of development through independent innovation will China's AI have the confidence to meet any containment or suppression) and that its development "不能筑起封閉的圍牆，而應搭建互利合作的橋梁" (must not build closed walls, but should build bridges of mutually beneficial cooperation) [386].

**15.** **Orders, customs and data centres.** Reuters, Sept 16, 2025: "Beijing has become increasingly keen for Chinese firms to switch to domestic chips even if Nvidia's products are preferred by many companies" [387]. By three people the Financial Times did not name, the cyberspace regulator "told companies, including ByteDance and Alibaba, this week to end their testing and orders of the RTX Pro 6000D", Nvidia's other chip made for China (Financial Times, Sept 17, 2025) [388]; Bloomberg reported the instruction the same day from people of its own [389]. An executive at one of the companies told the paper: "The message is now loud and clear"; "Now it's all hands on deck to build the domestic system." [388] "Teams of customs officers have been mobilised at major ports across the country in the past few weeks to carry out stringent checks on semiconductor shipments" (Financial Times, Oct 9, 2025, by three people it did not name) [390]. By two sources Reuters did not name, new data-centre projects that took state money were told "to only use domestically-made artificial intelligence chips" (Reuters, Nov 5, 2025), which added: "It is unclear whether the guidance applies nationwide or only to certain provinces, sources said" [391]; according to the unnamed sources in the same Reuters report, new data centre projects that had received any state funding and were less than 30% complete were told "to remove all installed foreign chips, or cancel plans to purchase them" (as the Taipei Times carries it) [392]. Reuters reported on Jan 8, 2026, as The Business Times carries it, by one person it did not name, that "Beijing in recent days asked some Chinese tech companies to temporarily pause their H200 chip orders as regulators are still deciding how many domestically produced chips each customer will need to buy alongside each H200 order" [393]. Later in January 2026, by people Reuters did not name, customs agents were told that the H200 chips "are not permitted to enter China", and companies were "explicitly instructed not to purchase the chips unless necessary"; Reuters wrote that it was unclear whether Beijing wanted to ban the chip outright, "is still chewing over restrictions, or whether these measures could be used as a bargaining tactic in talks with Washington" [394]. Leiphone, Oct 31, 2025, described a newly circulating document on window guidance (窗口指导, the same term as the Financial Times's window guidance in point 14): "有最新流传的“窗口指导”相关文件写道". By its account, projects "有补贴等地方力量参与" (in which local forces such as subsidies take part) "应全部使用国产芯片" (are to use home-made chips only); projects not far along "可要求对国外芯片进行拆除" (may be required to remove foreign chips); "新建的市场化项目，则被明确禁止使用H20等国外减配版GPU" (new market-based projects are expressly barred from the H20 and other cut-down foreign GPUs); and local projects outside the national hub clusters get no subsidies [395]. No copy of the document was found: the article gives no issuer, number or date, and neither web searches for its words, such as "应全部使用国产芯片", nor the titles on the state planner's and the finance ministry's lists of documents turned one up. The state planner's Li Chao, of its policy research office, did speak in public the same day, using the same term for the hub clusters: new large and very large computing facilities are to be sited first within the "国家枢纽算力设施集群" (national hub computing clusters); that answer has no rule on chips [396]. The official texts found that describe the window guidance of 2025 give it as a rule of the state planner, with the industry ministry and the data administration, on approving, siting and funding computing centres, with no rule on chips: a city's reply of June 2025, "2025年4月，国家、省相继下发新政策，要求对算力中心建设实施“窗口指导”" (in April 2025 the state and the province, one after the other, issued new policies requiring window guidance for building computing centres) [397], and, by the same reply, "算力设施建设由其他社会资金出资建设的不纳入“窗口指导”范围，采用项目备案制度" (computing facilities built with other social funds are not within the scope of the window guidance and come under project filing) [397], while Tongliao's data bureau says in a reply whose URL carries a November 2025 date that the rules put all large computing infrastructure under it whoever invests, "服务器采购金额10亿元以上的，全部纳入窗口指导" (server purchases of 1 billion yuan or more: all within the window guidance), and smaller projects only where the government or local state firms invest [398]; a city data bureau's Party group names the three bodies, "根据国家发展改革委、数据局、工信部联合对算力中心实施“窗口指导”的要求" (by the requirement of the National Development and Reform Commission, the data administration and the industry ministry, which jointly apply window guidance to computing centres) [399]; a province's data bureau, Liaoning's, in a reply of Jun 24, 2026, says "当前算力中心已被列为“两高”项目并受国家发改委“窗口指导”政策严格约束，明确规定地方政府不得对算力建设环节进行任何财政补贴政策" (computing centres are now listed as “two highs” projects, high in energy use and emissions, and are strictly bound by the state planner's window-guidance policy, which expressly bars local governments from any fiscal subsidy for building computing) [175]; and a city plan names the notice, "《关于对算力基础设施实施“窗口指导”的通知》（发改高技〔2025〕320号）" [400]. An industry body gives the guidance a point on chips: the data-centre committee of the China Communications Industry Association, in a report of 2025 it compiled with companies and others, lists among China's important computing policies the window guidance for computing infrastructure projects, of March 2025, with the state planner, the industry ministry and the data administration as its issuers, as covering "已建成、在建、拟建算力中心" (computing centres built, being built and planned) and as asking that "算力中心需明确采用芯片的类型、数量、国产化率等指标" (a computing centre must state such indicators as the type, number and home-made rate of the chips it uses) [401]; that is a summary, not the notice's text, and as summarised it sets no share and bars no chip.

**16.** **The result.** Reuters, May 14, 2026, by three people it did not name: Washington had cleared about ten Chinese firms to buy the H200 (in a later New York Times account relayed by Central News Agency on Nov 9, 2026, people familiar with the matter said the U.S. Commerce Department appeared to have cleared about 24 Chinese firms to buy or handle it [402]), but "not a single delivery has been made so far", three unnamed sources told Reuters in its May 14, 2026 report carried by the New Straits Times. A fourth unnamed source said scrutiny in China had "intensified after the State Council issued two recent supply chain security regulations" [403], and, by one person, "Chinese firms pulled back after guidance from Beijing"; the same person said the shift "was partly triggered by changes on the U.S. side, though exactly what changed remains unclear", and Reuters wrote that the "path to a completed sale has been obstructed by a tangle of requirements on both sides" [404]. Reuters, Nov 5, 2026, by two people it did not name: "About 600,000 of Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips are stored in Hong Kong awaiting shipment to China", "as Beijing withholds approval for large-scale imports despite demand from technology firms" [405]. Luckey's 600,000 matches this report. What Washington did with the licence applications is on the record, not reported. The table of licence applications for the H200 and AMD's comparable MI325X, with their status on May 11, 2026, from the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS), was made public on Nov 19, 2026 by the House Foreign Affairs Committee, under its chairman's powers and by the committee's "unanimous consent" [406] [407]. The table marks every approved application "Approved, not shipped", and 1,888,950 of the 3,444,160 chips in it, 55%, are in applications returned without action [5] (the table by status is in section 4).

The returned applications include every one for more than 75,000 chips and all 1,118,500 chips meant for places in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand; no approved application is for more than 75,000. The committee adds that some applications are "not fully reflected in the May 11, 2026, summary document": "For example, NVIDIA applied on December 19, 2025, to export 445,000 H200s to Tencent" [6]; the table shows an Nvidia application of that date for Tencent at 75,000 chips, approved, its only Tencent row of that date; neither document says whether that row is the application of 445,000. So the 55% is a share of the table, not of everything applied for: it would be 49.5% if that row is the application, counted at 445,000, and 48.6% if the 445,000 is a further application and was not returned without action (my arithmetic). The table gives no reasons; RWA is the bureau's own short form: "If your original application was returned without action (RWA)" [270]. Each row names a data-centre operator or ultimate consignee: an application is not an order, and an approval here is Washington's licence, not Beijing's. Below its rows the table adds that BIS "has granted two licenses which authorize various chips, including the H200, to go to Lenovo, but which do not authorize the chips to go to China locations" [5]; they are not among the 54 rows.

## 4. The numbers

The rows count different things and are not one series. Four are on the record: the United States government's count (two rows) and Nvidia's own account (two). The rest come from press reports citing unnamed people, and two later accounts say otherwise of the first: Nvidia's chief executive, the day after the report of the approvals in the fourth row, said "我认为中国政府仍在决策中，我们尚未收到采购订单" (I think the Chinese government is still deciding; we have not yet received purchase orders), as Voice of America's Chinese service renders him [408], though at Nvidia's GTC conference of 2026, as Jiemian reports him, he said Nvidia had licences to supply H200 chips to many Chinese customers and "收到了可观的采购订单" (had received sizeable purchase orders) [409], and TechWeb, as Sina carries it, reported on Jul 17, 2026, from industry insiders it did not name, that "国内AI厂商几乎未对英伟达H200芯片下达批量采购订单" (China's AI firms had placed almost no bulk purchase orders for Nvidia's H200) [410]. An application in Washington's table names a data-centre operator or ultimate consignee; it is not an order, and approved there means an American licence, not a Chinese one.

| Step | Number | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Reported: ordered by Chinese companies for 2026 | "more than 2 million H200 chips" | Reuters, Dec 31, 2025 [233] |
| Applied for in Washington by May 11, 2026: the official count | "applications to sell 3,444,160 covered" chips, Nvidia's H200 and AMD's MI325X together, in the table's 54 rows (each status in the next table); by the summary's definition, "PRC companies" include entities whose "ultimate parent company" is headquartered in China | The House Foreign Affairs Committee's summary and the table of licence applications and their status, from BIS, made public by the committee's chairman on Nov 19, 2026 [6] [5] [406] |
| Approved by Washington by May 11, 2026: the official count | 879,868 in 21 applications, each marked "Approved, not shipped": 579,868 in Nvidia's 17 and 300,000 in AMD's four. None is above 75,000; Reuters, by two people it did not name, reported a limit for each approved buyer of Nvidia's H200: "each approved customer can purchase up to 75,000 chips"; a later report, the Financial Times's as Reuters relayed it, says Washington "cleared the companies to purchase up to 100,000 H200 chips each" (ByteDance and Tencent) [411] | The same [6] [5]; Reuters, May 14, 2026 [404]; Financial Times, through Reuters, Aug 18, 2026 [411] |
| Reported: approved by Beijing, with conditions | "more than 400,000 H200 chips in total" for ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent | Reuters, Jan 28, 2026 [412] |
| Reported: under discussion in July 2026 | "fewer than 200,000 in total" | The Information, through Reuters, Jul 8, 2026 [413] |
| Reported: delivered by August 2026 | "about 10,000 H200 processors" each for ByteDance and Tencent | Financial Times, through Reuters, Aug 18, 2026 [411] |
| Reported: stored in Hong Kong, November 2026 | about 600,000 | Reuters, Nov 5, 2026 [405] |
| Nvidia's own account, quarter to Jul 26, 2026 | shipments under the H200 licences "account for less than 1% of Data Center revenue in our most recent quarter" | Nvidia, Form 10-Q [329] |
| Nvidia's own account, quarter to Oct 25, 2026 | "Q3 sales of licensed Hopper Data Center products to China was $2.4 billion"; Reuters: "The Hopper family includes the H200" | Nvidia's results call, Nov 17, 2026 [414]; Reuters [415] |

| Status on May 11, 2026 | Chips | Applications | What the rows show |
|---|---|---|---|
| Returned without action | 1,888,950 | 9 | every application whose ultimate location is Malaysia, Indonesia or Thailand (1,118,500 chips; the operators or consignees are named for ByteDance, BytePlus, TikTok and Alibaba), and all five applications above 75,000 chips |
| Approved, not shipped | 879,868 | 21 | Nvidia 579,868 in 17; AMD 300,000 in 4; all for locations in China |
| Submitted | 600,342 | 23 | dated Dec 23, 2025 to Apr 15, 2026 |
| Rejected | 75,000 | 1 | one AMD application, for UCloud |
| All | 3,444,160 | 54 | Nvidia 2,811,310 in 42; AMD 600,000 in 8; Semsotai USA, a third exporter in the table, 32,850 in 4, all for Sharetronic Data Technology and all still submitted |

The sums are this note's, of the table's 54 rows read from its page images; they match the summary's ten figures [5] [6]. The table marks returns RWA, "returned without action (RWA)" on the export bureau's pages [270], and gives no reason; one possible reason is that "The applicant has requested the application be returned" [416]; the export rules give another, "If a license application is registered, but BIS is unable to correct deficiencies crucial to processing the license application, it will be returned without action." [417]. The committee says some applications are "not fully reflected" in BIS's summary of May 11, 2026, such as Nvidia's of Dec 19, 2025 "to export 445,000 H200s to Tencent" [6]; the table's only Tencent row of that date is for 75,000, approved, and the documents do not say whether it is that application (point 16).

**Nvidia's share in China.** Reuters, in its own words: "Before U.S. export curbs tightened, Nvidia commanded about 95% of China's advanced chip market." [404] By the research firm IDC's data and its own sales, as the chip maker Enflame combines them in its prospectus of September 2026, about 4 million AI accelerator cards were shipped in China in 2025, Nvidia's 2.2 million of them, about 55%: "英伟达以约220万张出货量占据约55%的市场份额" [217]. By Reuters's account of IDC's data for 2025, which has the same total and the same figures for Nvidia, Chinese makers were "accounting for 41% of the total market" [418] [419]. For January to June 2026 IDC gives Nvidia "its 34% market share" and Huawei "a 28% share of the overall market" [420]. TrendForce, as reported in June 2026, counts differently: the foreign suppliers' combined share "is expected to fall to 21 percent in China this year, from 34 percent last year" [421]. Bernstein, by the Associated Press's report of Jun 29, 2026, "estimated that Nvidia had about a 40% market share in China's AI chips market in 2025, roughly matched by Huawei" [422], where IDC's data give 55%. These are different measures, not one series. Nvidia's chief executive told the Special Competitive Studies Project, as Tom's Hardware reported in May 2026: "In China, we have now dropped to zero" [423], which Tom's Hardware describes as a 0% share of China's AI accelerator market based on Nvidia's direct sales to Chinese customers. The record now holds six more counts, each of its own measure: IDC China vice president Zhou Zhengang's figures, as reported by Yicai, putting Nvidia at 65.2% and domestically made cards at 34.6% of China's data-centre accelerator-card market in 2024, against 85% to 90% for Nvidia in 2022 and 2023 [424]; KZ Consulting's figures putting the home-made share of China's AI accelerator-card shipments at 34.5% in 2024 and 41.3% in 2025 [425]; CCID Consulting's, in an article carried by Sohu, that home-made AI chips shipped in the first half of 2025 passed 600,000, their "市场份额从2023年（生成式AI发展元年）的14%提升至35%" (share up from 14% in 2023 to 35%) [426]; a report of China Telecom's research arms citing CAICT, that Nvidia's H100, H200 and other chips hold "国内大模型训练芯片市场的60%以上" (more than 60% of China's market for chips that train large models), with no period given [427]; Bernstein's estimate of China's AI semiconductor production capacity, as a Mitsui institute gives it, "10,000 wafers per month" in 2025 [428]; and The Wall Street Journal's report, from people it does not name, as World Journal relays it, that Huawei's 2026 AI chip shipments were estimated at about 1.5 million, and that its planned production of about 750,000 Ascend 950 chips was "仍远低于市场需求" (still far below demand) [429]. Huawei's rotating chairman, Eric Xu, gave a reading with no figure at a media roundtable on Sept 17, 2026, in Huawei's own English summary: "Right now, it's pretty hard to collect data about the market share of NVIDIA in China. But based on the data we have collected, Ascend has surpassed NVIDIA." [430] The Chinese summary is more guarded, "昇腾应该已经超过了英伟达" (Ascend should already have passed Nvidia) [431], and Reuters, in its report as The Edge Malaysia carries it, quoting him as saying "I think Ascend market share should be bigger than Nvidia's", wrote: "Huawei did not provide data to support the estimate." [432] In the same session he said Huawei did not have "enough capacity to even satisfy the demand in China", and of its supply, already "supply at scale" by Huawei's past, set against the demand for AI infrastructure: "I think it is very little" [430].

**A Chinese chip maker's revenue.** Cambricon's filings show the other side of the same years: its revenue was 25.7 million yuan in the first quarter of 2024 and 3,530.8 million yuan in the third quarter of 2026 [433] [434]. The company itself lays the fall in its revenue of the first half of 2024 partly on an American listing, "受“实体清单”等供应链不利因素影响" (affected by the Entity List and other supply-chain troubles) [435], and dates the start of batch deliveries: its main cloud products "2024 年第四季度开始向客户批量交付" (began to be delivered to customers in batches in the fourth quarter of 2024) [436]. Its five largest customers, whom it does not name, took 88.66% of its sales in 2025 [437]. Another maker, Enflame, names its largest end customer: its sales to Tencent Technology (Shenzhen), directly and under the corresponding AVAP sales model, were "33.34%、37.77%和 83.79%" of its operating revenue in 2023, 2024 and 2025 [256] [438].

**Nvidia's own revenue from customers based in China.** This is from Nvidia's reports to the Securities and Exchange Commission, not from the press. Nvidia counts revenue by where its direct customer has its headquarters. For China, Hong Kong included, the line was $9,659 million in the quarter ended Apr 27, 2025 [349], $2,973 million in the quarter ended Oct 26, 2025 [4], $7,880 million in the quarter ended Jul 26, 2026 [329] and $11,050 million in the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026 [439]. So it fell by about two thirds during 2025 and then rose: the latest quarter is the highest of the nine reported on this basis, and 9.5% of Nvidia's revenue of $115,844 million (the division is mine). The two fourth quarters in that run of nine are my subtraction of nine-month figures from annual ones [348] [4]. The line is not a count of chips entering China. Nvidia's note on the table: "The end customer and shipping location may be different from our direct customers' headquarters location." [439] It covers every product; the then interim chief executive on CNBC, Nov 17, 2026 (Nvidia's Board reinstated the chief executive on Nov 22, 2026 [440]): "Our shipments to China are approximately about 10 percent. But that is our gaming business. That is our automotive business. That is our workstation business that is going there." [441] For the next quarter the filing says: "We are not assuming any Data Center compute revenue from China in our outlook." [439] Point 19 gives Nvidia's words on its call.

**Customs figures.** China's imports under the customs line for parts and accessories of computers were US$82.2 billion in January to September 2026, against US$18.9 billion a year earlier [442] [443]. The line is a total for a kind of goods and names no maker, so it cannot count Nvidia chips. Nvidia's own list puts its H200 NVL and H20 NVL16 parts in subheading 8473.30, parts and accessories of computers, and its HGX H200 and HGX H20 parts in 8471.80, which this table does not show on a line of its own [444].

## 5. Five limits on the claim

**17.** **Beijing was not one actor.** Vice Premier He Lifeng told Nvidia's chief executive in April 2025 that more American firms, Nvidia among them, were welcome to put down deeper roots in China: "欢迎包括英伟达公司在内的更多美资企业深耕中国市场" [445]. The commerce minister told him in July 2025 that he hoped multinationals, Nvidia among them, would give Chinese customers high-quality, reliable products and services: "希望包括英伟达在内的跨国公司为中国客户提供优质可靠的产品和服务" [446]. Nvidia's chief executive, asked in August 2025 about China's scrutiny of the H20, said, by the Wall Street Journal's report as Mint carries it: "They requested and urged us to secure licenses for the H20s for some time." [384] That is his account, and he does not say who asked. The Financial Times, Nov 13, 2026, describes the split: the commerce ministry "favoured imports", while security officials in the cyberspace regulator and the security ministry "viewed dependence on Nvidia as a strategic vulnerability"; "Over the past year, the latter position has gained ground." [447] No other outlet was found reporting this split from sources of its own; the paper had described the like on Aug 20, 2025 [248]. The US Trade Representative said in November 2026 that the Chinese side is not monolithic (point 12). In the same days as the first of the summer's instructions (point 14), People's Daily Overseas Edition (Jul 22, 2025) printed an interview in which a university scholar, Zhang Xin, advised that "国内企业的最优策略是“双线并行”" (the best strategy for Chinese firms is two lines at once): "一边积极申请、储备进口高端芯片" (on one side, actively apply for and stock imported high-end chips), on the other fill the computing gap with innovation in algorithms and the home-made ecosystem [448]. That is a scholar's printed advice, not an act of the state. On May 14, 2026, meeting the American executives who came with the President, Xi said: "中方欢迎美国对华加强互利合作，相信美国企业在华将拥有更广阔前景" (China welcomes more mutually beneficial American cooperation and believes American firms will have broader prospects in China) [18]. Premier Li Qiang told them, Nvidia's among them, that a stable and open policy environment is the Chinese government's long-term commitment that will not change: "一个稳定开放的政策环境是中国政府长期不变的承诺" [449]. The record now holds six more cases of officials welcoming or discussing cooperation with Nvidia or another American AI-chip maker (counted by script), among them: the commerce ministry's vice minister Wang Shouwen to an Nvidia executive on Nov 25, 2024, "中方欢迎英伟达继续扎根中国，共享发展机遇" (China welcomes Nvidia's continuing to take root in China and share in its opportunities) [450]; Shanghai's mayor Gong Zheng to Nvidia's chief executive on Apr 18, 2025: "欢迎英伟达等全球知名高科技企业把握中国超大规模市场和上海扩大高水平对外开放的机遇" [451]; the trade-promotion council's account of its chairman Ren Hongbin discussing AI-industry cooperation with Nvidia's chief executive on Jul 15, 2025 [452]; and, in a newspaper report carried by the Jiangsu Party committee's news site, Wuxi's mayor Jiang Feng to AMD's chief executive on May 20, 2026, hoping to cooperate "在共建智算集群、开源生态建设、完善产业配套等方面" (in jointly building AI computing clusters, developing the open-source ecosystem and improving industrial support) [453]. Reuters, Jan 30, 2026, reporting that DeepSeek too had been approved: "China's industry and commerce ministries have granted approvals for all four companies" (DeepSeek, ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent), with the conditions left to the state planner [454]. By The Information's report of Sept 27, 2026, which Reuters relayed and could not verify, it was the industry ministry that told some companies the government intends to approve their purchases of the RTX PRO 5500 (point 19) [455]. The commerce ministry also proposed to ease Chinese firms' computing abroad, inside a new control: presenting on Oct 16, 2026 a draft that adds 23 AI technologies, computing clusters among them, to the catalogue of technologies whose export is prohibited or restricted, it said exports of technology for "中国企业在境外自建自营算力中心" (computing centres that Chinese firms build and run themselves abroad) would get "适用自动许可等便利措施" (automatic licences and other facilitation) [456], while the announcement of the commerce and science ministries that carries the draft at the same time put the technologies it lists under a temporary registration before export, "并同步对相关技术实施出口前登记管理" (and at the same time applies registration before export to the technologies concerned) [457], and the draft's items on computing clusters are for clusters with "人工智能加速卡总数超过10,000张" (more than 10,000 AI accelerator cards in all) [458]; and on Nov 17, 2026 it called the new American curbs on remote access interference in the "正常贸易合作" (normal trade cooperation) of Chinese firms with firms of other countries [459]. Local voucher rules paid for computing that is not home-made too: of the 93 rules found by searches that went province by province and city by city, saved and read and counted (not a census of every local government), 81 state no higher rate for home-made computing and 8 pay a lower rate than for home-made; 4, Sichuan's, Ningbo's, Huangyan district's and the Changsha zone's, pay only for home-made (point 3). Nine of those 81, though, also give home-made computing money to build, buy or run its facilities, an award for adapting to it, or a condition on suppliers, by another provision of the same rule: a premium beside a payment that stays open to other computing (point 3). Some state-linked bodies named Nvidia's chips for computing projects in their own words: Sinomach, which Reuters calls a state-owned engineering group [460], carried on its site on Feb 11, 2025 its design institute's word that the institute had won a 3-billion-yuan computing-centre project in Xi'an that is to use "英伟达H100、H200算力芯片" [461], and an undated listing of a county's investment service centre on the Fujian commerce department's investment site says the computing centre that is the first of two phases of a project seeking investors will have its computing servers "全部采用H100的服务器" [462]; neither page says how the chips would be obtained. Two more such cases are now held: Sinomach again, a year earlier, of a centre in Henan it designed that "部署国际一流的H100、H200智算服务器" (deploys H100 and H200 servers) [463]; and 航锦科技, which calls itself "武汉国资旗下上市公司" (a listed company under Wuhan's state assets), of the "上海 NVIDIA L20 集群灯塔项目" (Shanghai NVIDIA L20 cluster project) it delivered [464]. Local governments' own pages, too, name Nvidia's chips for computing centres and platforms, mostly beside home-made ones: Haidong's city government office, in Qinghai, in the plan for a zero-carbon computing park it issued on Apr 22, 2025, lists a centre of "128 台 H800GPU 服务器" (128 H800 GPU servers) [465]; a project that the Jilin commerce department's pages list for investors on Apr 27, 2026 plans "3600P 英伟达 H100 稀疏算力与 2400P 国产算力" (3,600P of Nvidia H100 sparse computing and 2,400P of home-made computing) [466]; a news item of Mar 20, 2026 credited to the government of Huangnan prefecture, in Qinghai, has a centre begun on a "“国产信创+英伟达”双芯架构" (two-chip design, home-made Xinchuang and Nvidia) [467]; Linyi's city government, at a press conference on Dec 31, 2025, said its computing marketplace is adapted to "NVIDIA、华为昇腾等多种类型算力设备" (Nvidia, Huawei Ascend and other kinds of computing equipment) [468], and Jiangsu's data bureau, in a report of June 2026 with three partners, that the province's computing dispatch platform is adapted to Cambricon's and Huawei's chips and to "英伟达类型 A100、H800、V100 GPU" (Nvidia's A100, H800 and V100 GPUs) [469]; and a weather-forecasting centre for the Greater Bay Area, on the Shenzhen meteorological bureau's site, asked on Sept 9, 2025, in a call for comment on its needs, for one card whose "核心型号为NVIDIA H200" (core model is the NVIDIA H200) [470]; these are plans, a listing, a project begun, platforms and a call for comment, not purchases, and none says how the chips would be obtained. The record now holds 11 more local governments' and zones' own pages of this kind (counted by script), among them a county development and reform bureau's in-principle approval of a project to build a centre with "64套英伟达高性能GPU服务器" (64 sets of Nvidia high-performance GPU servers), "资金来源为上级资金及自筹资金" (funding to come from higher-level and self-raised funds) [471], the Xiong'an New Area's scheduling platform, which the area's website says initially coordinates 23,000P of computing capacity, including "非国产化算力达16000P" (16,000P not home-made) [472], and Weihai's human resources bureau's agreement with Zhongke Xinkong to offer a free month of computing to local high-level talent who need it. A Lingang district official said the Weihai computing centre operated by Zhongke Xinkong "依托国际领先的英伟达芯片" (relies on Nvidia's chips) [473]. So the conflict ran through the state itself: its security and planning side against the companies, with the commerce side nearer to the companies, and with local governments and the carriers' own units on both sides of it: some pressed for home-made computing (points 3 and 5), others welcomed Nvidia, approved or offered computing on its chips, or used it.

**18.** **Guidance and public warnings, not a published ban.** No published Chinese order against buying Nvidia chips was found in the 82 policy pages read. Such an order could have been found: the 2023 review conclusion telling operators of critical information infrastructure to stop buying Micron's products is among those pages, "应停止采购美光公司产品" [351]. And when the finance ministry issued a purchase bar by name, on Jun 22, 2026, "不得采购46家美国企业（不包括在华美资企业）生产的产品" (government buyers may not buy products made by 46 American firms, leaving out American-funded firms in China), its list gave only the 46 firms' names, Lockheed Martin's first, and named none of the chip makers searched for (Nvidia, Intel, AMD, Micron, Qualcomm, in English and in Chinese) [474] [475]. Nor was a published rule on importing these chips found. China's lists of goods that need an import licence, an automatic import licence or a dual-use import licence, for 2024, 2025 and 2026, read whole by script, name no computer, computer part or chip (no customs number under headings 8471, 8473 or 8542, and none of five words for them), though the same search finds the computers and chips that the dual-use list controls for export [476] [477] [478]; nor do the lists of banned imports in force [479]; the sixth batch, of 2005, lists 17 kinds of asbestos and chemicals [480]. The licence lists set their scope by how the goods are named and described, each in a note of its own; the import-licence list's reads "进口许可证管理的商品范围以商品名称描述为准，商品编号供通关申报参考" [476], and no description names them either (each list with its saved copy: tables/import_lists_read.tsv). Nor does any customs or tariff text that a second search of Nov 25, 2026 reached and read whole: the announcements of the customs administration and of the Customs Tariff Commission found for January 2024 to November 2026, with their annexes, set no licence, hold or chip-specific inspection on importing these goods, and none names Nvidia or a chip model (the texts read: tables/customs_and_tariff_texts_read.tsv; not a census of every numbered announcement). Apart from the inspection of used goods on import, which reaches computers and integrated circuits of any origin (the list of 2015 of used goods inspected lot by lot at the port has "8542.3100-8542.9000 集成电路及微电子组件" (integrated circuits and microelectronic assemblies) [481], and a Shenzhen customs office fined an importer in July 2026 for graphics-card modules found to be used, "属于旧机电未报检" (used electromechanical goods not declared for inspection) [482]), the one published import measure found that could reach American chips is a duty that goes by origin, "对原产于美国的所有进口商品，在现行适用关税税率基础上加征34%关税" [483] (from Apr 10, 2025), and the China Semiconductor Industry Association, on Apr 11, 2025, read customs' rule on the origin of integrated circuits as the place where the wafer was made: "根据海关总署的相关规定，“集成电路”原产地按照四位税则号改变原则认定，即流片地认定为原产地" [484]; by that rule a chip that an American firm designs and a foundry outside the United States makes is not of American origin. That retaliatory duty, 125% by the time of CNN's report of Apr 25, 2025, had, by import agencies that spoke to CNN, been waived, without an announcement, on eight kinds of American integrated circuits, memory chips left out: "tariffs on eight kinds of integrated circuits, covering most semiconductors except for memory chips, had been waived to zero", while the customs administration and its offices in Shenzhen and Zhongshan "said they were not aware of the exemptions" [485]; Fibocom Wireless, a maker of wireless modules, says the like of its own buying in its Hong Kong listing prospectus of Oct 14, 2025, its American-origin baseband and radio-frequency chips "獲豁免中國關稅" (exempt from Chinese tariffs) from Apr 24, 2025 and its American memory chips taxed, and names no announcement for the exemption [486]. The purchase rules in point 2 bind Party and government bodies at township level and above and two kinds of public institution tied to them, and the same sentence adds "其他单位可不在采购需求中提出此项要求" (other units may leave this requirement out) [19]. No published national plan found sets a home-made share for computing centres: every such share found in a plan is a province's or a city's. A reply on the Suzhou (Anhui) municipal government site describes a designation condition in the science ministry's trial guide for national next-generation AI public computing open innovation platforms. The reply says the guide requires that the nominal computing power from independently developed chips be at least 60% of the system's total nominal computing power, "基于自主研发芯片的算力标称值占全部系统算力标称值的比值应不低于60%". The reply gives home-made computing shares of 20.21%, 5.81% and 0% for three centres that did not meet the conditions. The zone did not proceed with the first application round in 2023. Firms were reluctant to invest because home-made computing was dear and market prospects were unclear. The reply adds that the designation would bring no national funding or project subsidy [487]. Two brokers' reports and a press article quote the same sentence of the guide, one of them dating the guide to June 2022 [488] [489] [490]; the ministry's own page of the guide was not found, so the condition is known from four other bodies' accounts of it. And the operations director of the National Supercomputing Center in Wuzhen told National Business Daily on Nov 3, 2026 that "国家对超算中心有核心设备国产化率75%以上的要求" (the state requires supercomputing centres to have at least 75% of their core equipment home-made) [491]; no published text of such a requirement was found. The national computing-network opinions of December 2023 set none, though they set one for green power, "国家枢纽节点新建数据中心绿电占比超过80%" (over 80% green power in new data centres at the national hubs) [492], and nor do seven later central texts read, up to the plan for AI and energy of April 2026, which asks to speed the fit of "自主智算芯片" (independent intelligent-computing chips) to home-made frameworks [493]. Two national texts among the 82 policy pages do hold a home-made percentage, of other kinds: the 20% price preference of point 2, and an advisory committee's roadmap of October 2015 for Made in China 2025, which expected home-made products in several fields to reach stated shares of the home market by 2025, among them high-performance computers and servers at "国内市场占有率预计超80%" (expected to exceed 80% of the home market) and mobile-terminal chips at 40% [494]; at its launch the industry ministry's vice minister said "路线图不是指令企业怎么做,而是指导性的、参考性的" (the roadmap does not tell firms what to do; it is guidance and reference) [495]. A draft national plan for AI data centres with "at least 80 per cent of the core technology, AI chips included" from local suppliers was reported by Bloomberg in June 2026, from unnamed people [496]. Asked on Jun 18, 2026 about foreign reports of large investment in a computing network, the state planner's Li Chao gave no investment figure and no home-made share, and said "在具体建设过程中，市场力量将起决定性作用；我们将会同有关部门，加强统筹协调和政策引导" (in the actual building, market forces will play the decisive role; we will, with the departments concerned, strengthen overall coordination and policy guidance) [497]. Bloomberg's report of September 2024 said it in terms, by people it did not name: "The policy has taken the form of guidance rather than an outright ban" [379]. The earliest reports saved of the summer's guidance said the same in August 2025 (point 14). The Financial Times's report of Sept 17, 2025 does call it a ban, in its headline, "China bans tech companies from buying Nvidia's AI chips", and in its text, "The ban goes beyond earlier guidance from regulators"; what it describes is an order told to companies, on the word of three unnamed people. It reports no published rule, and none was found [388]. Reuters's report of Nov 5, 2025 (point 15) has the word in its headline too, "China bans foreign AI chips from state-funded data centres, sources say"; its text speaks of guidance and of a directive, on the word of two unnamed people, and says "The sources did not identify which Chinese regulatory bodies had issued the order". It, too, reports no published rule [391]. The published texts found stop short of an order: the commentaries and the industry body's call in point 4 warn and urge, and none forbids. The state-media warnings came mostly in one burst, with a smaller group in December 2025 and January 2026: of 297 state and Party media items on Nvidia and its chips found from July 2025 to November 2026, 23 of the 34 that only warn are dated Jul 31 to Sept 24, 2025, the first six of the day of the summons, among them the cyberspace regulator's notice as CCTV's site and Xinhua carried it and a Global Times report in which an expert says that deploying the chips in computing centres risks moving algorithms abroad, the last a Science and Technology Daily report of a forum at which an academician urged government and enterprise users to speed up home-made substitution [151] [148] [152]; one is of Jul 24, 2025, a week before the summons, one of Nov 1, 2025, six of Dec 9, 2025 to Jan 14, 2026, in reports on the American easing for the H200, and the last three are in Global Times in March and November 2026, two an industry analyst's words in news reports and one the paper's own unsigned commentary (point 4), and the items after Sept 24, 2025 are what the searches and the scan of the saved pages found, a sample of what these outlets printed and not a full count (tables/state_media_items.tsv). Before the burst, a search back to January 2024 found one item that only warns, of March 2025 (point 4); of the 35 warnings found from January 2024 to November 2026, 23 fall in those eight weeks. In every answer found on Nvidia's chips or on American AI chips for China, Beijing's spokespeople confirmed nothing (31 exchanges from December 2024 to Sept 13, 2026: a search of Nov 25, 2026 read every regular press conference of the foreign ministry in those months, 411 by its count, and the commerce ministry's regular briefings it could reach; a second search that day added six exchanges at the commerce ministry's briefings, and a second reading of the foreign ministry's pages, by a written rule, added two, and a reading of Nov 26, 2026 one more at the commerce ministry's briefings, of May 29, 2025; each of the 31 answers is held and its words checked; and those since, below: the foreign ministry's 47 regular press conferences of Sept 14 to Nov 25, 2026 were read whole by the same rule on Nov 26, 2026, and no answer in them confirms or denies a restriction, an instruction or an approval): "我不了解你提到的情况" (I am not aware of what you mention), said the foreign ministry on Aug 13, 2025 [498] and the commerce ministry on Jan 22, 2026, asked whether the government had told companies it would approve H200 purchases only in special cases [499], and on Mar 19 and Jul 16, 2026 [500] [501] [502]; "具体问题建议向中方主管部门了解" (for specifics, I suggest you ask the competent Chinese departments), said the foreign ministry on Sept 18, 2025 [503], and in like words on Jan 29 and Nov 9, 2026 [504] [505]. It had answered so before, on Aug 22, 2025, to a question that cited reports that China had stopped Chinese companies using the H20, "此前有报道称中方阻止中国公司使用H20芯片": "这个问题建议向中方主管部门了解" (for this question, I suggest you ask the competent Chinese departments), neither confirming nor denying them [506]. Asked on Nov 3, 2026 about a Financial Times report that Nvidia required some data-centre partners to give at least 51% of their services to non-Chinese firms, the foreign ministry said "具体情况我不了解" (I do not know the specifics), adding that all sides should cooperate on fair, transparent and non-discriminatory terms [507]. Asked on Nov 12, 2026 by a reporter of Agence France-Presse, who said "位于西安的中国西北工业大学的采购记录显示，该校购买了英伟达H200芯片" (the procurement records of Northwestern Polytechnical University in Xi'an show that the university bought Nvidia H200 chips), it said, as recorded in the transcript carried by China's embassy in Laos, "你提到的具体情况我不了解" (I am not familiar with the specifics you mention) and that it opposed American discriminatory lists against Chinese institutions [508]. Asked on Nov 18, 2026 about Nvidia's statement that it would no longer supply Hopper data-centre products, the H200 among them, to China, the foreign ministry said only "中方此前已经多次阐明在美国输华芯片问题上的原则立场" (China has already stated many times its principled position on US chip exports to China) [509]; asked on Nov 23 about the President's saying he would sell China only older chips, and whether China was negotiating with Washington, it said "关于美国出口管制措施，中方此前已表明立场" (China has already stated its position on US export-control measures) [510]. A Hong Kong paper's account of a Financial Times report, Aug 19, 2026, says purchases "需通過國家發改委審批" (must pass the state planner's approval) [511]: an approval step is reported, though no rule for it was found. A 2020 antitrust decision even obliges Nvidia to go on supplying its GPU accelerators to China on fair, reasonable and non-discriminatory terms, "向中国市场继续供应英伟达GPU加速器", unless the conditions have since been lifted [512] (the regulator's rules of 2023 say a decision to lift conditions is to be made public, "市场监管总局评估后决定解除限制性条件的，应当及时向社会公布" [513]); the regulator's list of conditional decisions, read on Nov 24, 2026, has no notice changing or lifting them, while it carries notices lifting the conditions of two other deals and changing the party bound by a third [514]. One listed company, 智微智能 (stock code 001339), which buys AI servers made in part by foreign makers, told the Shenzhen exchange in September 2026 that it had compared them with three Chinese catalogues, of technologies banned or restricted from import, of goods under import licence, and of dual-use items and technologies under import and export licence: "经比对，智算服务器及配套设备不涉及目录项下禁止或限制进口的产品或技术" (on comparison, the servers and their equipment are not products or technologies banned or restricted from import under the catalogues); the reply names no maker of the servers it buys or of their chips (it names AMD only as the platform of terminal products it develops, and names chip firms it has invested in), and puts the risk to supply on other countries' export controls [515]; its notice on unusual share-price movements of Nov 12, 2026 says it buys Nvidia's Jetson modules for the edge and robot controllers it builds, a product line that accounted for under 1% of its total revenue in the first three quarters of 2026, "主要业务模式为公司向英伟达采购其芯片模组" [516]. Two more listed companies told the same exchange the like in 2026. 宏景科技 (Hongjing Technology, 301396), in its reply of March 2026, said of its buying of computing servers that there was "不存在违反中国现行法律法规中关于进口、运营算力服务器的禁止性规定的情形" (no breach of the prohibitive provisions of Chinese law in force on importing or operating computing servers) [517]. 首都在线 (Capital Online, 300846), in September 2026, said that it usually buys its GPU servers through suppliers in China, which handle their purchase, import and delivery, and that buying the servers for its computing-centre project that way "不违反中国现行法律法规的禁止性规定" (breaches no prohibitive provision of China's laws and regulations in force) [518]. Two more listed companies said the like that year: 彩讯科技 (Richinfo, 300634), whose reply of September 2026 puts servers made abroad at 83.16% of the equipment it plans to buy with a bond issue, buys them through suppliers in China and says that by Aug 31, 2026 it "未因上述管制政策在采购主要生产设备方面遭遇实质阻碍或禁运" (had met no material obstruction or embargo in buying its main production equipment because of those controls, the American ones it describes) [519]; and 行云科技 (Xingyun Technology, 300209), answering posts that its computing cards were stuck at customs, said on Aug 11, 2026 that since it began its computing business, none of its imported computing equipment or related goods had been "被海关扣留、滞留" (held or stranded by customs) [520]. So Luckey's "China will not give them an import permit" [1] says more than the record shows: no permit rule for these chips was found in the import lists read (above). The law does allow a restriction outside the lists: the commerce ministry, with the State Council's approval and on the grounds the law lists, may "临时决定限制或者禁止前款规定目录以外的特定货物、技术的进口或者出口" (decide for a time to restrict or ban the import or export of particular goods and technologies outside the catalogues) [521], in the words of the law as amended in 2022; the law as revised on Dec 27, 2025, in force from Mar 1, 2026, keeps the power, "临时决定禁止或者限制前款规定目录以外的特定货物、技术的进出口" (decide for a time to ban or restrict the import and export of particular goods and technologies outside the catalogues), and among its grounds are national security and "为建立或者加快建立国内特定产业的" (to build, or speed the building of, a particular domestic industry) [522]; no such decision naming these chips was found. The State Council's regulations on the import and export of goods, made under that law, say of temporary measures: "对进出口货物采取限制或者禁止的临时措施的，国务院外经贸主管部门应当在实施前予以公告。" (where temporary measures restricting or banning imported or exported goods are taken, the State Council's foreign-trade department shall announce them before they take effect) [523]. Reuters's words are that Beijing "withholds approval for large-scale imports" [405]. The 82 policy pages read are those of the project's note of policies checked, which has 103 entries on 83 saved pages; one of them, a Reuters report on MarketScreener (row 59), is a refusal page, and the report is read in a radio station's copy of the same article. What was found, then, is guidance, as the press reports it, and public warnings, not a published ban.

**19.** **Some purchases were reported approved, and Nvidia reported licensed sales.** Reuters, Jan 28, 2026: ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent "have been approved to purchase more than 400,000 H200 chips in total", by four people it did not name [412]. The same report says Beijing was "only granting approvals with conditions" and, by the same people, that these "were still being decided upon"; a set ratio of home-made chips for each purchase it gives only as "One proposal that Chinese authorities have discussed in the past" [412].
