Two reports of December 2025 had come before it, each citing unnamed sources: the Financial Times, Dec 9, as the news service MNI quotes it, that buyers "would probably be required to go through an approval process, the people said, submitting requests to purchase the chips and explaining why domestic providers were unable to meet their needs" [524]; and Reuters, in a Dec 22 dispatch, as Newsweek Japan carried its Japanese edition on Dec 23, that at an emergency meeting on Dec 10 (the English text of the dispatch, as Tiger Brokers carries it, has "emergency meetings earlier this month" [525] and no day) "中国企業のＨ２００購入に国産半導体も一定比率で加える提案があったという" (there was a proposal to add home-made chips in a set ratio to Chinese companies' H200 purchases) [526]. The conditions are reported two ways. Lianhe Zaobao, the same day, citing an earlier Bloomberg report: Chinese regulators "要求上述三家科技企业需采购一定比例的国产晶片" (required the three companies to buy a share of home-made chips) as one condition [527]. Bloomberg's report of Jan 23, by people it did not name, is of a step before purchase: regulators "granted in-principle approval for Alibaba, Tencent Holdings Ltd. and ByteDance Ltd. to move to the next stage of preparations for purchases" [528]; its own Japanese excerpt says the authorities will urge companies to buy home-made chips, and sets no amount: "当局は承認の条件として、一定量の国産半導体チップ購入を企業に促す方針だが、具体的な数量は設定されていない" (as a condition of the approval, the authorities intend to urge companies to buy a certain amount of home-made chips, but no amount is set) [529]. Three more things from those months bear on how far the approvals reached. Bloomberg had reported on Jan 8, 2026, from "people familiar with the situation", that officials were preparing to let companies buy the chip "for select commercial use", while "the H200 chip will be barred from the military, sensitive government agencies, critical infrastructure and state-owned enterprises due to security concerns" [530]: a line drawn round state and security users, though in the fuller text of the same report, as the Taipei Times carries it, "If these organizations still ask to use the component, applications would be reviewed on a case-by-case basis, the people added." [243]. Nvidia's chief executive said the day after Reuters's report that no orders had followed: "我认为中国政府仍在决策中，我们尚未收到采购订单" (I think the Chinese government is still deciding; we have not yet received purchase orders), as Voice of America's Chinese service renders him [408]. And when a senior executive vice president of Tencent, Dowson Tong, was asked on May 28, 2026 whether Beijing had approved Tencent's H200 purchases, he answered, in a Hong Kong news service's reported speech, "對於集團採購不同的芯片，中央政府是表示支持的" (as for the group buying different chips, the central government has said it supports this) [531]; a Hong Kong paper's account of the same answer adds that he could not answer on particular chip models, "但就未可答复具体的芯片型号" [532]. Nvidia's chief executive, in an interview on Bloomberg Television on May 18, 2026, put the choice with Beijing: "The Chinese government has to decide how much of their local market do they want to protect" [533], and added "My sense is that over time the market will open." [533]. Deutsche Welle's Chinese service renders a fuller sentence of his: "H200已获得对中国销售许可，但中国政府必须决定要保护本地市场到什么程度，以及他们希望透过更大的AI产能扩张本地市场到什么程度" (the H200 has its licence for sale to China, but the Chinese government must decide how far it wants to protect the local market, and how far it wants to grow it with more AI capacity) [534]. The Information reported on Jul 8, 2026, as the newsletter Trivium China relays it, that China planned to let its largest AI firms buy a limited number of Nvidia's H200 chips, "fewer than 200,000", "less than half what the companies requested early this year"; in the newsletter's words, "The chips are for training only – with Chinese processors used for inference.", and limited to processing "public data, not sensitive data such as Chinese customer information" [535]. Bloomberg's relay of the same report, as The Straits Times carries it, adds what the firms had to say: "The companies need to say how many chips they need – and why – to get approval, according to the Information." [536] Reuters, Mar 17, 2026, by sources it did not name: "Nvidia has won Beijing's approval to sell its second-most powerful artificial intelligence chips to China" [537]; in the same report "A Chinese company source said that they did not know if the Chinese government had given final approval, but that Nvidia had told them that they could now place purchase orders." [537]. Nvidia's report for the quarter to Jul 26, 2026 says the licensed sales "were restricted by the PRC government", that it took a $0.4 billion charge "as the demand for H200 products diminished", and then: "we have made a fraction of the allowed shipments under the USG's H200 licensing program" [329]. The Financial Times, through Reuters, Aug 18, 2026: "Small batches of Nvidia's H200 chips, one of the company's most powerful AI chips, have been allowed to enter mainland China"; ByteDance and Tencent "have each received about 10,000 H200 processors in recent weeks", while "Beijing wants them to keep the hardware outside mainland China", and regulators told companies they could ship the chips to Hong Kong and use them there [411]. The paper's own text, as Yahoo Finance carries it, says most of the chips, and gives the reason by one of its people: "Beijing wants them to keep most of these chips outside the mainland to support domestic chipmakers, one of the people said." [538] Tencent's chief strategy officer, Mar 18, 2026, spoke of buying "higher-end imported GPUs which are now becoming available again" alongside more Chinese-designed ones [539]; a Chinese record of the call's questions and answers has the same answer under his name, "采购重新恢复供应的高端进口GPU" (buying high-end imported GPUs whose supply has resumed) [540]. Nvidia sold $2.4 billion of licensed Hopper products to China in the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026 (section 4) and said on its call: "we are no longer shipping licensed Hopper Data Center products to China" [414]. It gave no reason; Reuters wrote that its then interim chief executive "did not explain why Nvidia had stopped shipping the processors or whether the halt would be permanent" [415]. She told CNBC the same day that the licensed H200s "went to China within this last quarter" [441]. Two saved news pages carry a statement of Nvidia's in their reports of the released licence table. The Standard of Hong Kong, in a page stamped Nov 20, 2026, 04:57 Hong Kong time, has Nvidia say "in a statement" that the approvals were "subject to strict conditions and limited volumes" and that "despite receiving some licenses, we have since stopped shipping H200s to China" [541]. The Daily Caller, in a page stamped Nov 20, 2026, 20:29 UTC, about a day later, quotes an Nvidia spokesperson at more length (the page says the spokesperson spoke to the Daily Caller News Foundation): "The review and licensing process spanned more than half a year, with approvals subject to strict conditions and limited volumes." and "Despite receiving some licenses, we have already stopped shipping H200s to China." [542] Neither page says on which day the statement was made, and it gives no reason for the stop either. None of these says how many of those chips crossed into the mainland. Other Nvidia chips were sold, or were reported to have arrived, too. Nvidia's annual report, of the H20 licences of August 2025: "We generated approximately $60 million in H20 revenue under those licenses." [348] iSoftStone's own record of its results meeting of Jun 25, 2026 says its Shanghai node built a new H20 inference cluster, "上海节点新建 H20 推理集群" [543]; National Business Daily reported on Oct 13, 2026 that a person close to the company confirmed 8,000 Nvidia cards of the 6000D model had arrived [544]; the company's own WeChat post in which the news first appeared was deleted, "推文内容已被软通动力删除" (the post has been deleted by iSoftStone), and, called by Cailian Press's reporter as an investor, "公司人士未正面回应" (the company's people did not answer directly) [545]; a staff member told Yicai's reporter the same day that he was "对此事不太清楚" (not very clear about the matter) [546]. Two universities posted notices to buy that card in July 2026, each holding suppliers to the government procurement law's conditions: Beihang, asking for quotes for four, "品牌 NVIDIA 规格型号 RTX PRO 6000D" [547], and Wuhan University, by competitive bidding, for the "国行合规RTX PRO 6000D型号" (the compliant China-market model) [548]; a notice to buy is neither a purchase made nor a delivery. The older H20 was awarded in September 2025 and asked for by name in 2026: the University of the Chinese Academy of Sciences' award, published on its own purchasing site on Sept 28, 2025 and signed by the purchasing centre for central state bodies on Sept 26, lists six Dell servers, each with "8块GPU/FPGA/加速卡-NVIDIA HGX H20 8-GPU SXM 96GB 500W GPUs Assembly" [549], beside four servers each with "8*Ascend 910B4" [549]; an award is a contract decision, not a delivery. Shanghai Jiao Tong University's procurement intention, signed May 9, 2026, as a tender site carries it, plans for July 2026 to rent "6~8 台英伟达 H20 八卡 GPU 服务器" [550] (six to eight eight-card Nvidia H20 servers); and Wenzhou University's procurement intention, signed Oct 27, 2026, plans "一台基于NVIDIA H20 GPU的高性能计算服务器" (one high-performance server based on the NVIDIA H20 GPU) for 1.04 million yuan, to be bought in December 2026 [551]; an intention is not yet a tender. Result notices of universities name other Nvidia models: Northwestern Polytechnical University's school of cyber security awarded on Jan 28, 2026 the rental of servers listed with "NVIDIA HGX H200 8-GPU SXM 141GB" modules, among others [552], and its school of management bought two cards, "NVIDIA H200 141G PCIe显卡2片", for 874,700 yuan on Oct 21, 2026 [553], both read in Internet Archive copies, as the schools' own pages no longer open; in November 2026 Fudan University bought four cards listed as "英伟达RTX Pro6000计算显卡" (Nvidia RTX Pro6000 compute cards) [554], and Southeast University two GPU cards whose model column reads H100, with the supplier's own name in the brand column [555]. Each is a contract decision for a few cards or one server's rental, not a delivery. The record now holds 27 further notices found, of September 2025 to November 2026, not a census, of universities, research institutes, a hospital, a fund manager, an insurer's branch and a postal branch, that name Nvidia cards or ask for them by model, among them Shanghai University's plan for computing on "不低于8卡Nvidia H20（141G版）" (Sept 2025) [556]; Peking University Health Science Center's correction notice (bids due in Nov 2025), which removes the earlier starred term "单张GPU显卡类型：NVIDIA的H/A系列显卡" [557]; an award listing "NVIDIA-RTX-5880-Ada" cards for a buyer identified as zycgr21071303 (bid opening scheduled for Sept 2025) [558]; China Post's Zhejiang branch's tender, which lists "≥8块NV RTX PRO 6000 服务器版" alongside AMD GPU specifications (tender documents available in June 2026) [559]; the Southern University of Science and Technology's result for "英伟达（NVIDIA）RTX PRO 5000 Blackwell 72GB*2" [560]; and the AI institute of Hefei's national science centre, seeking quotes to rent "8 卡 H20 （ 96GB 版）裸金属 GPU 服务器" in November 2026 [561]; most are calls and tenders, for a few cards or a rental. On the RTX PRO 5500, the card of the orders in point 7, The Information, through Reuters, Sept 27, 2026, by two people: the Chinese government "has signaled it could allow" some companies to buy the card; the industry ministry "recently asked companies including ByteDance and Alibaba to report their plans to purchase Nvidia's RTX PRO 5500 chips" and "told some Chinese companies the government intends to approve the purchases"; Reuters "could not immediately verify the report" [455]. That is an intention reported, not an approval given. The Next Web's account of the same report has the ministry asking "how many of the chips they plan to buy, and what for" [562]. Asked about the report, an Nvidia spokesperson said: "US firms continue to be restricted by a combination of outdated US export controls, which cover gaming products released nearly a half a decade ago, and China's own limits on US imports" [455]. A paper of the Institute for Progress in Washington, dated Oct 19, 2026, which argues for limiting China's access to American AI chips, says of Chinese firms' orders for the card: "Chinese regulators are reportedly reviewing whether this demand is necessary" [563]; it gives the Financial Times as its source for the orders, in the sentence before, and names none for this. That Financial Times report, of Oct 13, 2026, "China faces uphill battle to wean tech firms off Nvidia chips" [564], is held only in a Chinese translation by Taiwan's Economic Daily News; there one unnamed senior executive of a large Chinese technology company says the regulators are asking firms whether their demand for the card is really necessary, and expects the answer yes: "監管機構正與科技企業溝通，了解他們對輝達RTX Pro 5500的需求是否確有必要。坦白說，答案會是『有必要』。" (the regulators are talking with the technology companies to learn whether their demand for Nvidia's RTX Pro 5500 is really necessary; frankly, the answer will be that it is) [565]. Reuters, Nov 23, 2026, by two people: some Chinese customers "have begun canceling orders" over concerns that the card "may not be permitted into China", and "Reuters could not determine the scale of the cancellations or whether the US or China would restrict shipments"; by two people too, large purchases by internet companies such as ByteDance and Alibaba "could require Chinese government approval" [566]. That report names no buyer, and none of the three listed buyers of point 7 had filed a delivery or a cancellation by Nov 25, 2026 (their notices on the disclosure site cninfo.com.cn, Oct 1 to Nov 25). By these reports and records, taken together, some purchases were reported approved, on the word of people the reports did not name, and Nvidia, by its own filings and calls, made licensed sales to China.

**20.** **The companies had reasons of their own, and American curbs and conditions pushed too.** Their own chips and costs first. Alibaba says its "chip design subsidiary T-Head has brought proprietary GPUs into scaled production" [567], and its chief executive said on the results call of Aug 20, 2026, in Chinese on the company's own record, that their wider use "避免了外购高端的商业化 GPU 的高额溢价对毛利的侵蚀" (has kept the high premium on bought high-end commercial GPUs from eating into gross profit) [568]; he judged in November 2026, "结合当前形势" (in light of the present situation), that from 2027 on "公司绝大部分新增AI芯片供给将以自研平头哥国产芯片为主" (the great majority of the company's added AI chip supply will be mainly the home-made T-Head chips it develops itself), with other home-made chips bought for part of its inference [569]; a transcript of the call on a stock-data site (where these two answers stand under the questioners' names, with no speaker label of their own) has that judgement after the words "在2027以及2028年，在2027之后" (in 2027 and 2028, after 2027), and, in the answer to a question on the new restrictions of the American commerce bureau, that in future most models "都会完全依赖于国产，国产芯片去做整个的训练" (will rely entirely on home-made chips for all their training) [570]. Baidu's chief executive said in April 2025, in Reuters' words, that it had switched on a cluster "comprising 30,000 of its self-developed, third generation P800 Kunlun chips" [571]; Baidu's own developer site says a Baidu Cloud Kunlun P800 cluster, switched on at 10,000 cards in February 2025, was enlarged that April, "该集群规模进一步扩展至 3.2 万卡" (the cluster was further enlarged to 32,000 cards) [572]. The founder of 360, Jul 23, 2025: its recent purchases "都是华为的产品" (are all Huawei's), and for inference "国产芯片性价比更高" (home-made chips are better value) than the H20; he granted that they trail Nvidia's but said they must be used, "只有咬牙坚持用" (only by gritting one's teeth and sticking with them) [573]; on TMTPost's podcast, he said that home-made cards are fine for inference and that training, for now, need not be forced onto them: "美国的先进武器还是可以借用一下" (America's advanced weapons can still be borrowed for a while) [261] (point 8). Zhipu, on the US Entity List since Jan 16, 2025 [574], says that in coding work the per-token inference cost on home-made chips "has reached a level comparable in cost-performance to mainstream imported GPUs", and gives its own reason: "Given the persistent global supply constraints on compute, this path ensures that the Company's cost curve remains within its own control" [575]. Huawei's rotating chairman gave the seller's reading of its buyers, in Huawei's summary of his roundtable of Sept 17, 2026: "For any customers, their decision to deploy Huawei solutions is made based on considerations about geopolitics and multi-vendor strategies. All companies must look to the long term, and try to ensure their supply security." [430] China Daily, counted among the state media of point 4, in a report of Aug 12, 2025, in the weeks of those warnings, quoted an engineer at an internet company it did not name, and printed the words again on Aug 15: "Cybersecurity is of crucial importance to us. We cannot tolerate any potential risks. Even if H20 chips are available now, we are not that eager to buy." [576] [577] Reuters, Sept 16, 2025, by two people it did not name: the RTX PRO 6000D (Reuters writes RTX6000D) met "only lukewarm demand", being "seen as expensive for what it does", in a report that also says "Beijing has become increasingly keen for Chinese firms to switch to domestic chips even if Nvidia's products are preferred by many companies" [387]; the Financial Times, the next day, by three people it did not name: "Several companies had indicated they would order tens of thousands of the RTX Pro 6000D" [388]. The record now holds 37 further cases of domestic chips and computing power, their use and costs, 30 of them on the record (documents, filings and named people's words), not in press reports: National Business Daily reported that Meituan's chief executive said on Aug 28, 2026, that its training and inference capabilities based entirely on domestic computing power "也帮助我们在长期成本以及基础设施可控性上建立了更强优势" (have also helped us build a stronger advantage in long-run cost and control of our infrastructure) [578]; Alibaba says its own chips give it "a cost and performance edge in the agentic AI era" [579]; a cloud firm, Capitalonline, Apr 10, 2026, that its customers' acceptance of home-made computing "已从被动合规逐步转向主动选择高性价比方案" (has moved step by step from passive compliance to actively choosing good-value solutions) [580]; Zhongbei Communications, in a prospectus of July 2025, plans mixed clusters for "国产芯片的成本优势及进口芯片的性能优势" (home-made chips' cost advantage and imported chips' performance advantage) [581]; Yicai reported that Baidu's head of cloud said on Nov 13, 2025, that "我们现在绝大多数的推理任务跑在P800上" (most of our inference now runs on the P800) [582]; and, the other way, a high-tech zone of Suzhou in Anhui said its firms showed little willingness to invest, citing "国产化算力价格昂贵" (home-made computing is expensive) [487]. American curbs pushed too, and they are the cause the record names most often (the further cases below; point 11 found one annual report in fifteen that names a Chinese authority's request). Reuters reported in November 2023 that Baidu had ordered Huawei chips that August, ahead of new US curbs, "to prepare for a future where they may no longer be able to purchase from Nvidia" [583]. Tencent's president, Martin Lau, said the same month of the new curbs: "And we will also try to look for domestic source for these training chips." [584] Early in 2024, before the guidance The Information reported in May (point 13), the cut-down H20 met reluctance too: Reuters wrote on Feb 1, 2024, in its own words, that Huawei's 910B "has become more popular amid concern that buyers could be faced with further restricted access to Nvidia's products resulting from U.S. sanctions" [585], and, by the Wall Street Journal's report as Asia Financial relayed it in January 2024, "China’s Tencent and Alibaba told Nvidia that they will purchase only a few AI chips from the US chipmaker", the two preferring home-made chips such as Huawei's [586]; a server maker, Inventec, expected in January 2024, as Cnyes reports it, that "預估第二季中國 H20 開始放量" (H20 shipments to China would begin to rise in the second quarter) [587], and a cloud firm, Capitalonline, said in its September 2024 investor relations record that, under its longer-term plans for China, "我们的产品对英伟达芯片的选型主要以 H20、L20 为主" (it planned to use mainly the H20 and L20 among Nvidia chips for its products) [588]. In November 2026 Tencent's president said the new US curbs on computing abroad left everyone in China with less advanced compute (point 9), and that they would hit training while, for inference, the "domestic supply chain should be able to cover it" [268]; Kingsoft Cloud, on its call of Nov 24, 2026, in the interpreter's English as a stock-data site transcribes it, put itself outside their direct reach: "we currently do not operate computing resources overseas, and so we are not directly affected by the new rules" [589]. Alibaba, in the results announcement dated Nov 19, 2026 that it filed in Hong Kong, wrote of the Commerce Department's interim rule of Nov 16, 2026 on remote access to advanced semiconductors, in the English version: "The Rule, as written, will impact our company's ability to access compute from certain providers outside China." [590]; the Chinese version of the same announcement has may where the English has will: "該規則（按現有條文）可能會影響本公司從某些中國以外提供商獲取計算資源的能力" [591] (the rule, as now written, may affect the company's ability to obtain computing resources from certain providers outside China). The US-listed Chinese companies whose annual reports speak of chip supply put the risk on export controls (point 11), and the three listed buyers of cards named PRO 5500 name overseas technology controls, and no Chinese approval, among the risks to delivery (point 7). A Chinese chip maker's prospectus says "地缘政治倒逼相关国内客户使用国产 GPU 产品" (geopolitics forces these domestic customers to use home-made GPUs), right after a list of American export rules [592]. Hanna Dohmen, a research analyst at Georgetown University's CSET, in her written statement for a US-China commission hearing of Feb 6, 2025: "export controls are creating increased demand for indigenous Chinese equipment, fabrication capacity, and AI chips" [266]. The commission's own report of November 2026, of the state: "U.S. and allied controls did not drive China's indigenization efforts, but they have added urgency in recent years." [189] The record now holds 24 further cases, 16 of them on the record (documents, filings and named people's words), not in press reports. The following disclosures, opinion and interview describe restrictions on access to chips or servers and their effects: Maixinlin, of 85 imported servers ordered in July 2024, "美国对高端算力服务器进出口实施更为严格的制裁措施，导致供应链受阻" (the US imposed stricter sanctions on imports and exports of high-end computing servers, disrupting the supply chain) [593]; Huina Technology, Apr 25, 2026: "受美国人工智能芯片对华出口管制等因素影响，内嵌英伟达人工智能芯片的高性能运算服务器供应愈发紧缺" (under US export controls on AI chips to China, among other things, high-performance computing servers with Nvidia AI chips grew ever scarcer) [594]; the sponsor of Zhejiang Zhonghe Technology, Jul 29, 2026: "公司项目研发所依赖的英伟达方案因国际技术管制导致断供" (the Nvidia solution the project relied on was cut off by international technology controls) [595]; Sugon Data, of the first half of 2025: "受到美国 H20 出口管制许可影响，出现放缓趋势" (construction of computing centers in China showed signs of slowing under US H20 export licensing controls) [596]; UCloud, in a prospectus of July 2026: "美国持续收紧对中国高性能 AI 服务器出口的限制" (the US has kept tightening restrictions on exports of high-performance AI servers to China) [597]; and DeepSeek founder Liang Wenfeng, in an interview carried by 36Kr: "我们面临的问题从来不是钱，而是高端芯片被禁运" (the problem we face has never been money, but the embargo on high-end chips) [598]. Nvidia, in every filing since November 2025: "we are already effectively foreclosed from the China market by U.S. export controls" [4]. The record now holds five further statements of Nvidia's own on its China business, among them its chief executive in Beijing on Apr 17, 2025, as China News Service reports him: the tighter US controls "已对英伟达业务产生重大影响" (have had a major effect on Nvidia's business), and as an American company it "必须遵守和严格执行相关政策" (must comply with and strictly carry out the policies) [599]. The H200 also met obstacles on the American side. The Commerce Department's rule of Jan 15, 2026 sets the conditions under which a licence application moves from a presumption of denial to case-by-case review; among them, the applicant must certify that "the item undergoes independent, third-party testing in the United States" and that "the aggregate shipments of the product to China and Macau will be no more than 50% of the total product shipped to customers for end use in the United States of that product" [271]. Nvidia: chips shipped under the licences "are subject to a 25% tariff upon importation into the United States", and "We have been unable to pass along any of the tariff to our customers" [329]. Its spokesperson in February 2026, of the US licence conditions: "the conditions need to be commercially practical, else the market will continue to move to foreign alternatives" [600]. Reuters, relaying the Financial Times, Feb 3, 2026: Chinese customers were "not placing H200 chip orders with Nvidia until it becomes clear whether they will be able to secure the licences" [601]. And Washington returned without action the applications for 55% of the chips its summary of May 11, 2026 records, among them every application whose ultimate location is Malaysia, Indonesia or Thailand, the operator or consignee of each named for ByteDance, BytePlus, TikTok or Alibaba (section 4). Asked on Nov 20, 2026 whether he would let "these chips go to China", the President said: "No, we want only older chips and we don't mind if none go, but only older." (a commercial transcript) [602] The record now holds six further accounts of U.S. export conditions for AI chips, among them the Commerce Department's own release of Jan 13, 2026, which says that applicants for licences to export the Nvidia H200, AMD MI325X and similar chips to China must show that those exports "will not reduce global semiconductor production capacity currently available to U.S. customers" and that the Chinese buyer "has adopted export compliance procedures, including customer screening" [603]; Nvidia's chief executive at CES on Jan 6, 2026, saying, as Central News Agency reports, that Nvidia was working with the US government to confirm the final details of export licensing for the H200 [604]; and a home-made chip maker, Hygon, Dec 10, 2025, on the H200's "附带销售分成推高采购成本" (attached revenue share raising the purchase cost) [605].

**21.** **What Beijing meant by it is not settled.** No Chinese document found states a motive for the unpublished guidance to companies, or ties a Beijing act against Nvidia to the trade talks. Beijing's own texts give purposes for its public acts: the cyberspace regulator summoned Nvidia "为维护中国用户网络安全、数据安全" (to safeguard Chinese users' cyber and data security) [327]; an unsigned article on the work of the market regulator's Party group says the regulator's cases against Nvidia, Qualcomm and DuPont China "有力维护国家利益" (forcefully safeguarded national interests) (point 10) [330]; and the regulator's annual report for 2025 lists the further investigation among cases that served "维护公平竞争" (to protect fair competition) [337]. Several American bodies read parts of Beijing's conduct as leverage in the trade talks or as retaliation: the US-China commission in November 2025, which reads the finding against Nvidia as leverage; the White House on Nov 1, 2025, which speaks of retaliation against American chip makers and other major American companies and names none of them; and the Trade Representative's report of March 2026, which calls China's antimonopoly investigations retaliation (point 10) [332] [333] [335]. Among China's commitments in the Busan deal the White House listed: "End Chinese retaliation against U.S. semiconductor manufacturers and other major U.S. companies." [333] The Congressional Research Service, September 2025, read the pressure as a push for access to more advanced chips: China "may use its purported security concerns about the H20 to press for technology disclosures and access to more advanced chips, such as Nvidia's B30 chip", and the market regulator's finding of that month was "another effort to pressure Nvidia to provide its advanced chips to China" [606]. The Financial Times reported a request of another kind on Aug 10, 2025, by several people familiar with the matter, as a newsletter excerpts the report: "Chinese officials have told experts in Washington that Beijing wants the Trump administration to relax export restrictions on high-bandwidth memory (HBM) chips", which, by people familiar with the matter, "seriously constrain the ability of Chinese companies, including Huawei, to develop their own AI chips" [607], and, according to one unnamed person cited by the Financial Times in a report excerpted by MP-IDSA, the Chinese team headed by Vice-Premier He Lifeng had raised the HBM issue in some of those negotiations [608]; and the Wall Street Journal, as Mint carries it, read Beijing the same way in its own words, on Aug 23, 2025: officials in Beijing were "reluctant to have Trump’s approval of the H20 treated as a U.S. concession to Chinese wishes", and "They say that if the U.S. really wants to show goodwill, it will give Chinese users more access to cutting-edge American technology" [384]; and on Nov 4, 2025, after the Busan meeting: "For Xi, the summit ended without achieving a key near-term objective: a concession on the U.S. chip ban." [609] Senator Tim Kaine said on the Senate floor on Dec 18, 2025 that the administration had first claimed it would permit only "inferior chips, such as the Nvidia H20, not the H200", "but China declined. They waited, believing that President Trump would fold, and that is what happened." [610] He gave no source, and did not say whether by China he meant the government or the companies. The Trade Representative's words point the other way, in three interviews with Bloomberg, two on Bloomberg Television and one on a Bloomberg podcast (captions, not checked by ear). Of Washington's H200 decision: "That was not a negotiated outcome." (Dec 19, 2025, 5:35) [611] Whether Chinese companies may buy is "a sovereign decision for China", and "They're very committed to domestic production." (May 15, 2026, 5:59 to 6:27) [8] Asked whether Xi raised chips on the President's trip: "But the Chinese side did not raise this issue with us." (Nov 20, 2026, 0:28 to 0:34) [366] The Treasury Secretary gave a third reading on Aug 13, 2025. The premise was the interviewer's: asked about a report that Beijing was sending firms letters telling them to "shrug off those H20", he did not confirm the letters and said "it it also tells me that they are worried about the Nvidia chips becoming the standard in China" (his words are from a caption track, not checked by ear) [612]. Two other remarks concerned Huawei's potential dominance of the Chinese chip market: Kevin Hassett said on Sept 25, 2025, as Central News Agency reports him, that China wanted "讓華為壟斷所有在華晶片銷售" (to let Huawei monopolise all chip sales in China) [613], and David Sacks said on stage at Salesforce's Dreamforce conference on Oct 14, 2025, "And what Huawei wants is for all American chips to be banned in China so they can dominate that huge market for themselves." (an excerpt The Minutes attributes to Salesforce and dates Oct 19, 2025, not checked by ear) [614]; the day is from the San Francisco Standard's report, published Oct 14, 2025, of his appearance on stage there that afternoon [615]. The American side had put chips on the table itself: asked why the April block on the H20 was lifted, the Treasury Secretary said "You might say that that was a negotiating chip that we used in Geneva and in London" (a clip of his Bloomberg Surveillance interview that Bloomberg's Balance of Power played on Jul 15, 2025; captions, 8:55) [616]; the same day the Commerce Secretary told Reuters, by CNN's report, that the resumed sales were part of the deal on rare earths: "We put that in the trade deal with the magnets" [617], and White House economic adviser Kevin Hassett had said separately on Jun 9, 2025 that the US could lift recently imposed export controls on goods such as semiconductors if China sped up delivery of rare earths and magnets, though not on "very, very high-end Nvidia stuff" (as recounted in a Jun 11 China Daily editorial) [618], though on Jun 11, 2025 the Treasury Secretary had told a Senate subcommittee "there is no quid pro quo in terms of chips for rare earths" [619]; and the President wrote on Dec 8, 2025 that he had told Xi the H200 would be allowed: "President Xi responded positively!" [620] That is the President's account, not Xi's words. The Dec. 8 entry in the 2025 Digest of Other White House Announcements summarises Trump's announcement as saying that Xi "responded "positively," with China agreeing to pay a fee of 25 percent on such sales" [621], where the post says "$25% will be paid to the United States of America" [620]; no Chinese text found says China agreed to pay. Whether the Nvidia case has ended is not known (point 10). As to the dates: the cyberspace regulator summoned Nvidia on Jul 31, 2025, two days after the Stockholm talks of Jul 28 and 29 [327] [622]; the antitrust finding came on Sept 15, the second day of the Madrid talks [328] [623]. By my count over the figure's own list (tables/talks_and_acts.tsv), 7 of the 13 restrictive acts and words fall within 7 days of a meeting in person, where about a quarter of all days do; the easing acts and words do not cluster (2 of 9, one of the two being the scholar's interview of point 17). But restrictions also followed deals: the order on state-funded data centres was reported on Nov 5, 2025, six days after the Busan meeting [391] [624], and the planner's guidance on home-made chips and the AI-chip evaluation list came a week and more after the Beijing summit of May 14, 2026 [147] [24] [625]. Some dates are those of press reports, the acts come in bursts, and a count of dates cannot show a motive. None of the 41 saved official pages on the talks (the two sides' readouts, joint statements and fact sheets, and six notices of a round to come) names Nvidia, the H20 or the H200, though the same search finds export controls in some; nor do the President's own posts on four calls with Xi and on two rounds. One Chinese official text sets the chip beside the talks: asked on Jul 18, 2025 about American officials' remark that approving H20 sales was part of the trade negotiations, the commerce ministry's spokesperson first described what followed the London talks and then said "美方近日又主动表示将批准对华销售英伟达H20芯片" (the US side has recently also said, of its own accord, that it will approve sales of Nvidia's H20 chips to China); the answer neither confirms nor denies that the chip was part of the talks [626]. Earlier, on Jun 2, 2025, the ministry's spokesperson had named American chip measures among those that, it said, broke the consensus of the Geneva talks, "包括发布AI芯片出口管制指南、停止对华芯片设计软件（EDA）销售" (among them issuing guidance on export controls for AI chips and stopping sales of chip-design software (EDA) to China), "严重破坏日内瓦经贸会谈既有共识" (seriously undermining the consensus already reached at the Geneva talks) [627]: it counts America's chip measures, not China's own acts, against the talks' consensus. Under each of the three readings the direction stands: what is open is what Beijing wanted from its acts, not which way they pulled the companies.

## 6. What can be said, and how

- **Flat, on documents and filings.** China's declared policy is to end dependence on foreign core technology and to raise the share of home-made chips, leaving room, in the leader's own words, for imported technology that is secure and controllable (points 1 to 3). Its regulators opened a case against Nvidia and summoned it (point 10). Nvidia told securities regulators in November 2025 that the Chinese government "restricted customers from purchasing, importing, or using our data center products" (the annual report says "discouraged" [348]), and, two sentences later, that "we are already effectively foreclosed from the China market by U.S. export controls" [4]. Alibaba told them the cyberspace regulator asked companies to halt purchases of certain chips (point 11). A Shanghai government body says internet companies' clusters in the city run mainly on Nvidia (point 5). By May 11, 2026 Washington had approved 879,868 of Nvidia's and AMD's chips, none yet shipped, and returned without action the applications for 1,888,950 (section 4). Nvidia's revenue from customers based in China fell by about two thirds during 2025 and was, in the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026, the highest of the nine quarters reported on that basis; that is not a count of chips entering China (section 4).
- **In public, as warnings and urging, not as orders.** The security ministry's warning, the state-media items that only warn (23 of the 34 found are dated Jul 31 to Sept 24, 2025, and six more Dec 9, 2025 to Jan 14, 2026; the items after September 2025 are what the searches and the saved pages hold, a sample and not a full count), the state broadcaster's account saying buyers could choose not to buy the H20, and an industry body's call for caution (points 4 and 18). Nothing read ties the state-media texts to the guidance the press reports, and a Party paper also printed a scholar's advice to stock imported chips (point 17).
- **As reported, from unnamed people.** Who gave the instructions to stop buying, and when; the warning about traitors; the customs checks; the 600,000 chips Reuters reported in Hong Kong; every number in the first table of section 4 except the United States government's count and Nvidia's own. Some of these rest on one outlet and some on more, each with its own sources: the warning about traitors on one (the Wall Street Journal), the port checks of October 2025 on one (the Financial Times), the rule for state-funded data centres on one (Reuters), the split inside Beijing on one (the Financial Times) and the 600,000 on one (Reuters); the pressure against the H20 in August 2025 on five, though they differ on whether firms were told to stop; the instruction on the RTX PRO 6000D on two (the Financial Times and Bloomberg); the H200 held by customs in January 2026 on two (Reuters and the South China Morning Post); and the go-ahead of late January 2026 on three, which Nvidia's chief executive disputed the next day.
- **As others read it.** That Beijing's acts were leverage in trade talks, or a sovereign choice with no bargain in them, or protection of the home market for home-made chips (points 19 and 21).
- **Not to be said.** That China banned Nvidia chips: no published ban was found, some purchases were reported approved, and Nvidia's own filings report licensed sales to China (point 19). That an import permit was refused: no permit rule for these chips was found in China's published import lists of 2024 to 2026 (point 18). That Beijing spoke with one voice: its commerce side and its security and planning side pulled apart (point 17). That the companies wanted only Nvidia: they also build and buy home-made chips. That the antitrust case shows Beijing wanted no Nvidia sales: by Nvidia's account the finding is that it offered Chinese customers degraded products. That the case was a bargaining move, or that it was not: both are readings (point 21). That only Beijing stood in the H200's way: Washington's conditions and a 25% tariff did too, and Washington returned without action the applications for 55% of the chips its summary of May 11, 2026 records. That Washington refused the applications it returned: the table gives no reason.

**One sentence that survives all five limits.** From 2024 the planning side of the Chinese state and the industry ministry, and from 2025 its security side, pressed China's AI companies, through unpublished guidance that the press reported, to buy home-made chips rather than Nvidia's, and from the summer of 2025 to stop ordering them, while the security ministry warned in public of backdoors in chips made abroad and state media warned about Nvidia's chips, most of all from the end of July to September 2025; published texts of provinces, cities and a region, and, by a city's reply, a science-ministry guide, set shares, quotas or conditions that favour home-made computing, none of them a ban; other parts of the state welcomed Nvidia and, by the reports, approved some purchases; the companies went on ordering Nvidia chips, in the millions by Reuters's reports, and the Financial Times reported in August 2026 that some H200s had been received, each outlet citing people it did not name; they were also turning to home-made chips, for reasons of their own and under American curbs; and whether Beijing meant its acts as lasting policy, as leverage in trade talks or as protection of its home market is read all three ways.

## What this note leaves open

- Palmer Luckey's clip has not been checked by ear, nor have the Trade Representative's words of Nov 20, 2026 (1:06 to 1:35), where the two caption tracks differ on one word and on where a sentence breaks.
- Five South China Morning Post articles are held only in their first paragraphs; none is cited in this note. Articles of The Information and Bloomberg are held through outlets that carry or relay them; Bloomberg's report of Jan 23, 2026 only in its English opening and its own Japanese excerpt, and its report on the RTX PRO 5500 (point 7) only in its own Japanese excerpt of Nov 4, 2026; other outlets' accounts of that report are saved and are not cited. Five Financial Times articles of 2025 cited here were saved by hand from the paper's own site on Nov 23, 2026 and read whole. The paper's report of Aug 10, 2025 on high-bandwidth memory is held only as a newsletter excerpts it [607], its report of Aug 12, 2025 only as a mirror of a syndicated copy, its report of November 2025 as The Irish Times carried it, its report of Feb 3, 2026 only as Reuters relayed it [601], and its report of August 2026 on deliveries as Yahoo Finance Canada carries the paper's text [538] and as Reuters and a Hong Kong paper relayed it. Its report of Oct 13, 2026 on the RTX PRO 5500 (point 19) is held only in a Taiwanese paper's Chinese translation; its own page, as the Internet Archive holds it, is a registration page.
- The Commerce Secretary's words are from a commercial transcript; a search found the same sentence in the committee video's own captions (1:17:28 to 1:17:47; a reading, with no saved copy). The committee's page for the hearing offers the video and his prepared remarks, and no transcript [363], as again on Nov 24, 2026 [628]; govinfo's list of the 409 Senate hearing prints of the 119th Congress issued or changed since Apr 22, 2026 holds none of this hearing [629].
- Nvidia's report for the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026 was not yet filed when looked for on Nov 23, 2026: the commission's record of the company's filings, fetched that day, lists none for that quarter [630], nor did it when fetched again on Nov 24, 2026, with filings up to Nov 23 [631], or on Nov 25, 2026 at 22:16 UTC [632]. Its commentary on that quarter says: "We plan to file our 10-Q in the coming weeks." [633]
- What Beijing meant (point 21): no Chinese official text found states a motive for the unpublished guidance or ties an act against Nvidia to the trade talks (the purposes stated for its public acts are security, national interests and fair competition). A Chinese news site's commentator did tie one to them: Guancha, on Aug 7, 2025, quoted its technology commentator, whom it does not name, as saying of the summons that "这一约谈时机还增添了中国在中美贸易谈判中的筹码" (its timing also added to China's bargaining chips in the China-US trade talks) [634], a commentator's reading, not the state's. No American written readout of the rounds in London, Madrid, Kuala Lumpur, Paris and Korea, of the round of September 2026, of the two leaders' own meeting in Shenzhen (the White House posted only the three leaders' declaration and a trip summary) or of the video calls of Dec 5, 2025 and Apr 30, 2026 was found in the Treasury's indexes of readouts and press releases, the Trade Representative's monthly lists of press releases or the White House's lists of its posts, as read on Nov 24, 2026; the Treasury's notices before four rounds and the President's posts after two name no chips. The foreign ministry's list of news on the United States names five calls between Xi and the President from January 2025 to November 2026; both sides' accounts of all five are saved, and none names chips. Why Washington returned applications without action: its table gives no reason.
- The window-guidance paper said to circulate in October 2025 (point 15): no text, issuer, number or date was found. The reported draft plan with 80% home-made technology (point 18) has not been published, and the planner, asked, did not confirm it.
- Local rules: the official page of Binjiang's rule of 2026 did not open, though the district political consultative conference's site carries the same measures word for word [141]; Sichuan's measures of 2026 were first held only as a news site carries their text; the provincial commission's own page is now held as the Internet Archive captured it [104]. Tianjin's voucher rule is held as Sina carries it [635], and the National Data Administration's site carries the same text with its signing date, Apr 27, 2025 [636]; of five cities' voucher rules sought, Suzhou's and Wuhu's were reached, Nanjing's only through an application guide, Qingyang's only through press accounts, and Chengdu's at first only through press accounts and then in a consultancy's full copy of its revised rules, compared by eye with the bureau's signed file, a scan [93]; a search of Nov 25, 2026 across the 31 province-level governments and 24 named cities and new areas added the rules listed in rows 55 to 102 of tables/local_vouchers_more.tsv, four searches of the same day that went city by city, each with a second pass, and readings of named and earlier rules added rows 103 to 216, a later reading of the same day added row 217 (Dalian's draft AI measures of April 2025, a draft, not counted), readings of Nov 26, 2026 added rows 218 to 227, and none reached the text of Chongqing's measures of August 2025, held only as their issuing notice in an Internet Archive capture [637]; the state planner's national voucher has no text of its own found; Zhejiang's development and reform commission, in its notice for 2026 payouts as a community account on Sohu reprints it, says "人工智能券（算力券）是国家使用超长期特别国债资金" (the AI voucher, or computing voucher, is a policy tool for which the state uses ultra-long special treasury bond money, as the sentence goes on to say), for card types including "所有自主智算算力及部分高性能非自主智算算力" (all independent intelligent computing and some high-performance intelligent computing that is not independent) [638], and Tianjin's rule names as its basis the national computing-network opinions of December 2023, which say "鼓励面向中小企业发放算力券、运力券" (encourage issuing vouchers for computing and for network capacity to small and medium-sized firms) [492].
- Public trade figures do not show how many of these chips entered China: each customs line is a total for a kind of goods and names no maker (yearly sums for five places in tables/trade_annual_summary.tsv). No saved page says under which line an importer in China or Hong Kong declares the chips. Nvidia's own classification list, "provided for informational purposes only", gives the subheadings it applies itself: 8471.80 for its HGX H200 and HGX H20 parts, 8473.30 for its H200 NVL and H20 NVL16 parts and 8471.50 for its DGX H200 systems [639] [444]. Singapore's own tables, read through its statistics service and not saved among the sources, show re-exports to mainland China under one line for computer units of about S$3.0 billion in July to October 2026, against about S$18 million in the first half; they name no goods or maker.
- The fuller record is in two files beside this one: OFFICIAL_RECORD.md (government documents and filings) and TIER2_REPORTING.md (reporting).
